Streaming and Massively Parallel Algorithms for Euclidean Max-Cut
Nicolas Menand, Erik Waingarten

Abstract
Given a set of vectors X = { x 1 , … , x n } ⊂ ℝ d X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{d} , the Euclidean max-cut problem asks to partition the vectors into two parts so as to maximize the sum of Euclidean distances which cross the partition. We design new algorithms for Euclidean max-cut in models for massive datasets: • We give a fully-scalable constant-round MPC algorithm using O ⁡ ( n ​ d ) + n ⋅ poly ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( n ) / ε ) O(nd)+n\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n)/\varepsilon) total space which gives a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximate Euclidean max-cut. • We give a dynamic streaming algorithm using poly ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ Δ / ε ) \mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon) space when X ⊆ [ Δ ] d X\subseteq[\Delta]^{d} , which provides oracle access to a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximate Euclidean max-cut. Recently, Chen, Jiang, and Krauthgamer [STOC ’23] gave a dynamic streaming algorithm with space poly ⁡ ( d ​ log ⁡ Δ / ε ) \mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon) to approximate the value of the Euclidean max-cut, but could not provide oracle access to an approximately optimal cut. This was left open in that work, and we resolve it here. Both algorithms follow from the same framework, which analyzes a “parallel” and “subsampled” (Euclidean) version of a greedy algorithm of Mathieu and Schudy [SODA ’08] for dense max-cut.



1
Introduction

This paper is on algorithms for Euclidean max-cut in sublinear models of computation, namely, massively parallel computation and streaming. Given a set of vectors, we consider the geometric graph consisting of
n
vertices (one for each vector) and all edges connecting two vectors with weights given by Euclidean distances. Euclidean max-cut asks to partition the vectors into two parts so as to maximize the sum of weights of edges cut by the partition. It is the natural geometric version of the classical max-cut problem on graphs, which has been well-studied from the approximation algorithms perspective. In general graphs, there is polynomial-time
0.878
-approximation
[
GW95
]
which is optimal assuming the Unique Games Conjecture
[
KKMO07
]
. For dense or metric instances of max-cut, there are known PTAS
[
dlV96
,
dlVK01
,
MS08
]
, which in particular, will play a prominent role in this work.

From the sublinear algorithms perspective, there has been recent progress on Euclidean max-cut in dynamic geometric streams
[
Ind04
]
, where a set of input vectors from
[\Delta]^{d}
are presented as a stream of insertions and deletions, and the algorithm must approximate (up to a multiplicative
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-factor) the Euclidean max-cut of the underlying set of vectors. In
[
CJK23
]
, the authors present a dynamic streaming algorithm which uses
\mathrm{poly}(d\log(\Delta)/\varepsilon)
space, and can approximate the
value
of the max-cut of the underlying set of vectors up to a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-factor. This algorithm improved on the prior work of
[
FS05
]
, who showed this was possible for constant dimension
d
(since space complexity scaled exponentially in
d
). As we detail in Section
1.2
, the algorithm of
[
CJK23
]
builds on a line-of-work on random sampling for max-CSPs
[
AdlVKK03
,
RV07
]
. Importantly, the algorithm approximates the value and not the cut itself; while a low-space algorithm cannot explicitly output a cut,
1
1

1

Since it is inherently an
\Theta(n)
-sized object, consisting of a partition of the vectors.
a low-space streaming algorithm could provide, after processing a set of vectors, oracle access of the form “which side of the cut is
x
in?” which produces an approximate max-cut. This was discussed in Appendix B of
[
CJK23
]
and left open, where they noted the algorithm of
[
FS05
]
along with
[
LSS09
,
Lam11
]
provides such oracle access, albeit with quantitatively much weaker
\exp\left(\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)\right)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log\Delta)
space.

Do there exists low-space streaming algorithms for high-dimensions (more generally, algorithms in sublinear models) which can, not only approximate the value of the Euclidean max-cut, but also provide oracle access to an approximately optimal cut?

The above question motivated this work. Here, we provide such algorithms which give oracle access to an approximate max-cut in the massively parallel model and streaming model of computation.

Approximating the value versus the object in high-dimensions.

Beyond Euclidean max-cut, multiple geometric optimization problems currently exhibit gaps in the complexity of approximating values and producing approximate solutions. Understanding the circumstances under which such gaps exists (or do not) is an interesting open problem, and one which has received recent attention. For example, such a gap (provably) exists for computing the Chamfer distance
[
BIJ+23
]
, and may also exist for the Earth Mover’s Distance
[
Roh19
]
and Euclidean minimum spanning tree
[
CS09
,
HIM12
]
. A potential gap may also exist in sublinear models of computation, such as in massively parallel computation and streaming. It has been recently studied for clustering and the facility location problem
[
CJK+22
,
CGJ+24
]
, as well as the Euclidean minimum spanning tree
[
CJLW22
,
CCAJ+23
,
JMNZ24
]
.

1.1
Our Contribution

In this work, we study algorithms in massively parallel and streaming models of computation which can provide oracle access to an approximately optimal Euclidean max-cut without paying exponentially in the accuracy parameter
\varepsilon
. In the model of massively parallel computation (MPC), a dataset of
n
vectors in
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
is partitioned across
m
different machines of space
s
(where
m\cdot s
is the total space used). The computation proceeds in rounds where machines communicate with each other. Upon termination of the algorithm, each point in the dataset should report which side of an approximately optimal cut it lies in. The following theorem appears formally in Section
3
as Theorem
5
.

Theorem 1

(MPC (Informal))
.

There is a
O(1)
-round fully-scalable MPC algorithm which outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate Euclidean max-cut using
O(nd)+n\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
total space.

In contrast with
[
CJK23
]
(which was designed for dynamic streaming, so applies in MPC), it can report an approximately optimal max-cut instead of solely the value. Alternatively,
[
FS05
]
does approximate the Euclidean max-cut, but requires the total space at least
n\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log n)\cdot\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon))
. As a point of comparison, the recent work of
[
CGJ+24
]
provides a constant-round
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
-approximation to Euclidean facility location in fully-scalable MPC with total space
O(n^{1+\varepsilon})\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d)
, and
[
JMNZ24
]
provides a
\tilde{O}(\log\log n)
-round, constant-factor approximation to the Euclidean minimum spanning tree in fully scalable MPC with total space
O(nd+n^{1+\varepsilon})
.

As we detail in Section
1.2
, Theorem
1
will come from designing a “parallel” and “subsampled” version of an elegant greedy algorithm for max-cut in dense instances
[
MS08
]
. This approach is a unifying theme throughout this paper, and Theorem
1
is the first (and simplest) application. Then, we show that this same framework gives rise to a low-space insertion-only streaming algorithm which gives oracle access to an approximate max-cut. More specifically, in insertion-only streaming, a dataset of vectors
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
is presented as a sequence of vector insertions; then, the algorithm provides oracle access to a function which determines, for each point
x_{i}
, which side of the cut it lies in. The following theorem appears formally in Section
4
as Theorem
8
.

Theorem 2

(Insertion-Only Streaming (Informal))
.

There is an insertion-only streaming algorithm using
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
space which provides oracle access to a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate Euclidean max-cut.

We emphasize that, conceptually, the proof of Theorem
2
is another application of the same framework used for Theorem
1
. We then turn our attention to the dynamic streaming model, where vectors in
[\Delta]^{d}
may be both inserted and deleted, and the streaming algorithm must provide oracle access to an approximate max-cut of the set of vectors which remain. Even though
[
CJK23
]
, could not provide access to the cut itself, we show that their main algorithmic primitive, “geometric sampling,” can be combined with our framework to provide access to the underlying cut.

Theorem 3

(Dynamic Streaming (Informal))
.

There is a dynamic streaming algorithm using
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
space which provides oracle access to a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate Euclidean max-cut.

Theorem
3
is strictly stronger than Theorem
2
and Theorem
1
, since the algorithm from Theorem
3
may be used (in a black-box fashion) in insertion-only streaming and massively parallel computation. The benefit to differentiating them is that the proof of Theorem
3
adds several layers of complexity in the analysis. As we describe in more detail, in massively parallel computation and insertion-only streams, we are able to simulate an algorithm where each point from the input dataset behaves independently—this independence is crucial to both Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
. The algorithm for Theorem
3
will forego the independence of the dataset points, and this necessitates re-analyzing our framework.

1.2
Technical Overview

As mentioned above, the streaming algorithm of
[
CJK23
]
builds on a line-of-work on random sampling for max-CSPs
[
AdlVKK03
,
RV07
]
. These works (tailored to max-cut) show that in any graph on
n
nodes, the max-cut value of
the subgraph induced by a random sample of
s=O(1/\varepsilon^{4})
nodes (re-scaled by
n^{2}/s^{2}
) gives an additive
\pm\varepsilon n^{2}
approximation to the max-cut of the graph with high (constant) probability.
[
CJK23
]
show the analogous result for geometric graphs; the
\pm\varepsilon n^{2}
approximation becomes
\pm\varepsilon T
, where
T
is the total sum of edge weights and is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-multiplicative approximation since the max-cut is always at least
T/2
. Instead of (uniform) random sampling of nodes as in
[
AdlVKK03
,
RV07
]
, one samples a node with probability proportional to the weighted degree (re-scaling is as in “importance sampling”). Their streaming algorithm takes “geometric samples” (developed in
[
CJK23
]
to sample points with probability proportional to the weighted degree, or
weight
), and finds the max-cut on the sample. The problem is the following: a max-cut on a sample does not necessarily extend beyond the sample. In this paper, we provide a method to do just that in streaming and massively parallel models: given “geometric samples” and a cut of those samples, provides query access to an approximately optimal cut of the entire dataset. Given a dataset point, we can determine whether it lies inside or outside a cut—the guarantee is that the resulting cut is a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate max-cut.

A Parallel and Subsampled Greedy Algorithm.

Our starting point is an elegant analysis of the following greedy algorithm of
[
MS08
]
for non-geometric graphs: given a graph
G
on
n
nodes, process nodes in a uniformly random order; for the first
t_{0}=O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
nodes, try all possible cut assignments, and for a fixed assignment of the first
t_{0}
nodes, the remaining nodes are iteratively assigned greedily; a node picks a side maximizing the contribution to already-assigned points. The fact that such extensions achieve an additive
\pm\varepsilon n^{2}
-approximation in (non-geometric) graphs is already highly non-trivial in classical models of computation; for sublinear models (like massively parallel and streaming algorithms) there are two immediate challenges to overcome:

•

Challenge #1: Random Order
. Processing the nodes in a uniformly random order is central to the analysis in
[
MS08
]
and directly at odds with the streaming model, whose inputs are received in potentially adversarial order.

•

Challenge #2: Space Complexity.
The assignment of a node depends on the assignment of “previously-assigned” nodes. This would seemingly require large space, or access to the input the moment a node is being assigned. In streaming, providing query access after fully processing the input means that neither large space nor access to the input is available.

As we describe next, we show how a “parallel” and “subsampled” algorithm can overcome both challenges in Euclidean instances. The parallel nature of the assignment rule will mean that, irrespective of the order in which points are processed, we are able to simulate a greedy algorithm. Furthermore, a greedy rule for assigning a point will pick the side of the cut which maximizes the sum of edge weights crossing the cut; since we cannot implement this greedy rule exactly, we use a sub-sample to estimate the greedy assignment. However, sub-samples cannot be independently drawn for each point assignment—if each point chose an independent sample, the overall space would still be too large. We proceed as follows: consider a fixed dataset
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
of
n
points in Euclidean space.

•

A (discrete) time axis begins at
t=0
toward an end time
t_{e}
. Each point
x_{i}
generates a timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{t_{e}}
: at each time
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
, it “activates” by sampling
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
with probability
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
, which is proportional to its weight.
2
2

2

Throughout the paper we use bolded variables to denote random variables. As we discuss later, we will have to decrease the weight as time proceeds, and hence the dependence on
t
on the weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
in Definition
2.2
.
A point assigns itself to inside/outside the cut the moment it is first activated (see Definition
2.2
). As in
[
MS08
]
, points activated by time
t_{0}
try all cut assignments.

•

Points activated after
t_{0}
are assigned greedily, but not by maximizing the contribution to all already-activated points. Alongside a timeline, each
x_{i}
samples a mask
\mathbf{K}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{t_{e}}
: at each time
t
,
x_{i}
is “kept” by sampling
\mathbf{K}_{i,t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma_{t})
. Then, a point
x_{i}
activated after
t_{0}
is assigned greedily by maximizing a weighted contribution to already-assigned points which were simultaneously “activated” and “kept” before time
t
, i.e., by considering points
x_{j}
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1
for some
\ell<t
(see Definition
2.3
).

The above is meant to address both challenges simultaneously (see Figure
1
). Because each point “activates” itself in a parallel manner, the order points are processed is irrelevant—each point behaves independently and checks its timeline and mask to determine whether or not (and when) it was activated and kept. The sum of all weights
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
will always be at most
1
, so we expect one point to activate at each time
t
, and
\gamma_{t}
points (which is less than
1
) to simultaneously be “activated and kept” at time
t
. Importantly, we only store points which are simultaneously activated and kept (which suffices, since our assignment only considers these points). Roughly speaking, we let
\gamma_{t}
be
1
at
t\leq t_{0}
and decay as
1/t
for
t>t_{0}
. As we will see, it will suffice for us to set an end time of
t_{e}=O(n/\varepsilon)
, so only
O(t_{0}+\log(n/\varepsilon))
points are stored in expectation. It remains to show that, even though we modified the assignment, a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation is recovered. Theorem
4
is the main structural theorem concluding this fact; assuming Theorem
4
(whose proof we overview shortly), the application to MPC and insertion-only streaming setup the data structures to simulate the assignment of Theorem
4
.

Figure 1:
Representation of the timelines for each point. The time axis is on the bottom, each each horizontal dotted line corresponds to the timeline of each point
x_{1},\dots,x_{10}
. Time
t_{0}
appears in the timeline with times up to
t_{0}
in gray. For a point
x_{i}
, a dot on the timeline corresponds to an activation, and a solid dot to an activation which is also kept. Up to time
t_{0}
, every point which is activated is also kept, but after that, points which are activated at time
t
are kept with probability
\gamma_{t}
. A point is assigned greedily whenever it is first activated; the assignment depends on the edge weights to the previously “activated” and “kept” points (i.e., those with solid black dots before the time of first activation). Hence, it suffices for algorithm to store points which are simultaneously activated and kept (i.e., those with solid dots).

Application to MPC.

The application to the MPC model is in Section
3
, where we show that one can simulate the parallel and subsampled greedy algorithm for Euclidean max-cut. In MPC, points are distributed across machines. First, each point computes its own weight (as well as the sum-of-weights) in
O(1)
rounds using
\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})
-sketches (Lemma
3.3
). Since each point computes its own weight, it can generate a timeline and mask according to Theorem
4
. In particular, each point
x_{i}
can generate the horizontal dotted line for
x_{i}
in Figure
1
and know which times have activated/kept (i.e., the solid/non-solid dots). If there exists a time
t\leq t_{e}
where a point is simultaneously activated and kept, it is communicated across all machines along with its weight (recall, there will be few of these). Letting
\gamma_{t}=1
for
t\leq t_{0}
, points activated before
t_{0}
are always kept. By taking a few additional samples, we give a procedure for the algorithm to try all possible assignments of the points activated by
t_{0}
, and to agree on which assignment to use (Lemma
A.2
). This procedure for agreeing on an assignment to points activated by
t_{0}
is shared in all of our algorithms. Once an assignment of the points activated by
t_{0}
is agreed upon, the remaining points assign themselves by maximizing a weighted contribution (as in importance sampling) to the activated and kept points (which were communicated to all machines).

Application to Insertion-Only Streaming.

The application to insertion-only streaming, Theorem
8
, follows a conceptually similar framework. We maintain a sketch which allows a point to compute its weight (and sum of weights) with respect to points inserted so far (Lemma
4.1
). With this weight, a point generates its own timeline and mask, and checks whether there exists a time
t
such that it is simultaneously activated and kept; if so, it remembers the point and the weight, and if not, then it “forgets” the point (Lemma
4.2
). Once more, one should keep Figure
1
in mind; the goal is for each point
x_{i}
to know its own dotted horizontal line, so it knows when it was assigned, and whether other point’s assignments depend on it (in case it is both activated and kept). An important caveat is that weights change: upon inserting a new point
x
, the weight of a prior point
y
changes. To address this caveat, we maintain timelines with respect to minimum weights so far, and thus, once we “forget” a point, it will not be simultaneously activated and kept when the weight changes in the future (because it always decreases). It turns out, the minimum weights under insertions cannot be smaller than a constant factor of the final weight (Claim
4.4
). At the end of the stream, we update the timeline of point remembered to a constant-fraction of the final weight (which still gives the desired
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation guarantee). In order to provide query access to the cut assignment of a point, we determine its (final) weight, and check its activation time in a timeline with respect to the constant-fraction of the final weight. We use Nisan’s pseudorandom generator, so that the algorithm can reconstruct the timeline and perfectly simulate the parallel and subsampled greedy algorithm.

Proof of the Theorem
4
: Parallel and Subsampled Greedy Algorithm.

For the application to MPC and insertion-only streaming, it remains to analyze the result of the greedy assignment produced by the above process. Our analysis will use a technique of
[
MS08
]
in defining a “fictitious cut” and adapting it to our parallel and subsampled process. Recall, the major differences are that (i) there is no underlying random permutation, but rather the
n
timelines generated by independently sampling activations from
\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
with different weights, and (ii) one cannot execute the greedy rule exactly, and one cannot even use independent sampling to estimate the greedy process; rather, there is one subsampling done (for the entire process) which is dictated by the mask
\mathbf{K}
.

The analysis begins in Section
5
, where we describe a (random) process,
GreedyProcess
, which iterates through
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
and generates the timelines and masks for each point, as well as produces the parallel and subsampled greedy assignment from Theorem
4
. We encode (partial) cuts of
n
points as
[n]\times\{0,1\}
matrices
z
with entries in
[0,1]
; the
i
-th row
z_{i}
is
(1,0)
in case it is assigned to the
0
-side,
(0,1)
in case it is assigned to the
1
-side, or
(0,0)
if unassigned; we let
f(z)
measure the sum of uncut edges and approximately minimize
f
.
As in
[
MS08
]
, we track a “fictitious cut”, is a
[n]\times\{0,1\}
matrix,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}
for
t=0,\dots t_{e}
, which evolves over time (Definition
6.1
). The fictitious cut will be defined so as the satisfy the following guarantees:

1.

The fictitious cut
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0}
begins by encoding the (optimal) max-cut
z^{*}
, and as
GreedyProcess
executes, becomes the output cut
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}}
. Each
x_{i}
and time
t
has
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i}
set (and forever fixed) the first time
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
is set to
1
(i.e., when it first activates). Hence, it suffices to upper bound
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})]
for each
t
, since the sum telescopes to
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}})]-f(z^{*})
.

2.

When a point
x_{i}
is first activated at time
t>t_{0}
, we set
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}
(and it is forever fixed) as follows. First, we evaluate

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma_{\ell}}\right)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j},

(1)

which gives the two numbers (recall
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j}
is the
n
-th row consisting of two numbers) and are interpreted as estimated contribution to either side of the cut. We set
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}
greedily to
(0,1)
if the first entry is larger, and to
(1,0)
if the second is larger.

3.

If
x_{i}
is not activated at time
t>t_{0}
, we also update
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}
. This update is solely for the analysis for a reason we explain shortly. It also appears in
[
MS08
]
in a simpler form (because points here are weighted), and we will see the weights add a few technical complications.

Since our goal is to upper bound
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})]
for each
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
, a natural approach is to fix an execution up to time
t-1
, and then to randomize (and take the expectation) over the randomness in
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
and
\mathbf{K}_{i,t}
for all
i
. By a simple manipulation, the change
f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})
can be expressed as a sum of two types of terms: type-A terms sum over
i
of
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1})\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j}^{t-1}
which measures, roughly speaking, the total weight change when a reassignment of
x_{i}
incurs, and type-B terms sum over all
i,j
of
d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1})(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j}^{t+1})
which accounts for points
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
which change simultaneously (expression starting in (
17
)). Type-B terms are easy to handle; roughly speaking, a single term
i,j
of
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1})(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1})
is large when
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
activate simultaneously, which occurs with probability
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{t}
and is small enough to upper bound the expectation (Lemma
6.3
).

The challenging terms are of type-A, and we informally refer to
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1})
as the
i
-th change, and
\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j}^{t-1}
as the
i
-th contribution. When
x_{i}
is activated, the new value of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}
is set greedily according to (
1
). Importantly, in order to evaluate (
1
), it suffices to consider those
j\in[n]
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1
for
\ell<t
(i.e., those which have been simultaneously activated and kept before time
t
), and this is the reason we only keep activated and kept points. On first glance, (
1
) appears to be an unbiased estimator for the
i
-th contribution (recall
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell})
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma_{\ell})
). If so, a greedy setting of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}
with respect to the
i
-th contribution guarantees type-A terms are all negative (Lemma
6.5
, whose analogous statement appears in
[
MS08
]
), and gives the required upper bound.

The important caveat is that (
1
) may not be an unbiased estimator for the
i
-th contribution because the fictitious cut
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j}
and the timelines and masks are not independent. The fix involves updating
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j}^{\ell}
when
j
is not activated at time
\ell
—this way, one can ensure (
1
) becomes an unbiased estimator (the second case of (
•
‣
6.1
) in Definition
6.1
). This also appears in
[
MS08
]
, albeit a different update which depends on the weight.
3
3

3

In the weighted setting, a technical issue arises for times when
1/\ell
is larger than the weight. In these cases,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}
may have entries which grow beyond
[0,1]
, which is a problem for Lemma
6.5
. Thus, we enforce
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\ell}=\min\{w_{i},1/\ell\}
when
x_{i}
has not yet activated.
In fact, a stronger statement holds showing that for any fixed execution of the first
t-1
steps, the expected error of (
1
) at time
t
, denoted
\mathbf{err}_{i}^{t+1}
, is smaller than the error of (
1
) at time
t-1
, denoted
\mathbf{err}_{i}^{t}
(Lemma
6.8
), which also occurs in
[
MS08
]
as the “martingale argument”. Thus, we account for type-A terms by upper bounding the expected deviation
|\mathbf{err}_{i}^{t+1}|
of (
1
) in Lemma
6.6
, which is where the fact
\gamma_{t}
cannot decay too rapidly comes in.

Incorporating the Mask.

If
\gamma_{t}
is always one and there is no sub-sampling (as in
[
MS08
]
), the additional error
|\mathbf{err}_{i}^{t+1}|
at each time
t
becomes
T/t^{3/2}
(in (
24
), set
\gamma^{\ell}=1
and the expression involving distances simplifies to
O(T)
by our setting of weights, Definition
2.1
). Once we sum over the
i
-th change,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i}
(Lemma
6.3
) and sum over all times larger than
t_{0}
, the total error becomes the sum over all
t>t_{0}
of
T/t^{3/2}
which is on the order of
T/\sqrt{t_{0}}
(before
t_{0}
, we try all possible assignments and incur no error). Notice that this is exactly analogous to
[
MS08
]
—the parameter
T
, which is the sum of edge weights corresponds to
O(n^{2})
in dense graphs, and
t_{0}=O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
would work. From (
24
), we can see that a smaller setting of
\gamma_{t}
would increase the variance (and hence the error incurred), but one can still control the error as long as
\gamma_{t}
does not decay too rapidly. For
\gamma_{\ell}=\gamma/\ell
in (
24
), we obtain an error per time step of
T(\sqrt{\gamma}/t^{2}+1/(t\sqrt{\gamma}))
(see Lemma
6.6
). This final expression is also
\varepsilon T
when
\gamma=\log^{2}(t_{e})/\varepsilon^{2}
and
t_{0}\geq\sqrt{\gamma}/\varepsilon=\log(t_{e})/\varepsilon^{2}
.

Dynamic Streaming Algorithm.

So far, our analysis proceeded by generating the timelines per timestep; in particular, Figure
1
considered the horizontal dotted lines: each point
x_{i}
independently samples the horizontal row
x_{i}
(according to the weight), and the space complexity is dominated by the number of simultaneously activated and kept points (the solid dots in Figure
1
). In a dynamic streaming algorithm, the underlying set of vectors (and hence, which horizontal dotted lines will appear) is only determined at the end of the stream. Instead, we focus on generating the columns of Figure
1
which will hold solid black dots (see Figure
2
). Specifically, we will use the “geometric sampling” technique of
[
CJK23
]
. A geometric sampling sketch is a small-space data structure which processes a dynamic dataset of vectors
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
and can produce a sample
\boldsymbol{x}\sim X
with probability proportional to the weighted degree of
\boldsymbol{x}
. In addition, it returns an accurate estimate of the probability
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}
that
\boldsymbol{x}
was sampled (for “importance sampling”). We use the following approach (Definition
7.3
):

•

We sample one mask
\mathbf{K}\in\{0,1\}^{t_{e}}
to determine which times will have points simultaneously activated and kept (instead of independent masks for each point). As in Definition
2.3
,
\mathbf{K}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma_{t})
, where
\gamma_{t}=1
for all
t\leq t_{0}
, and
\gamma_{t}=\gamma/t
for
t>t_{0}
.

•

If
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
, no point is activated and kept at that time and we set
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
for all
i
. If
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, we use a geometric sampling sketch to sample
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\sim X
, and set
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
and for
j\neq i
,
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
.

Each (individual) entry
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}
is distributed as desired (although no longer independent),
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma_{t})
. The points which are simultaneously “activated” and “kept” are recovered by the algorithm (since these are generated by the geometric samples). Because the assignment of a point
x_{j}
also depends on the first time it is activated, i.e., the first
t_{j}
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,t_{j}}=1
, we maintain a sketch to provide query access to the weight of a point—this allows a point to generate the remaining part of the timeline (the horizontal dotted lines in Figure
2
). With that, all ingredients are set (Subsection
7.2
): while processing the stream, we prepare
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
geometric sampling sketches, and a sketch to later query weights; once the stream is processed, we generate a single mask
\mathbf{K}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
and whenever
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, we use a geometric sample to determine which point is simultaneously “activated” and “kept” at time
t
. On a query
x_{j}
, we use the sketch to estimate the weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{j}
and generate the remainder of the timeline
\mathbf{A}_{j}
; this specifies the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{j}
, which is used to simulate the “parallel” and “subsampled” greedy assignment. The final piece is ensuring the cut output is still a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation (Theorem
16
), even though timelines are no longer independent, and the probabilities that geometric samples output are estimates, as opposed to the exact probabilities used. The proof of this final theorem appears in Section
8
.

Time Axis
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Figure 2:
Representation of the timelines for the dynamic streaming algorithm. Right above the time axis on the bottom, we sample one mask
\mathbf{K}
which determines which times will contain points which are simultaneously activated and kept—these are the solid black dots on
\mathbf{K}
. Times before
t_{0}
activate and keep a single point, and after
t_{0}
, points are kept with probability
\gamma_{t}
. Whenever
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
(with a solid black dot), the rectangle above it represents a geometric sampling sketch used to determine which point is activated and kept. After geometric sampling sketches are generated, the horizontal dotted lines represents the (remaining) timelines to be generated for each point.

1.3
Related Work

In relation to our streaming algorithm, this paper pursues a line of work initiated by
[
Ind04
]
in designing dynamic streaming algorithms for high-dimensional geometric problems. These have been extensively studied especially in the last few years. These include, in addition to Euclidean max-cut
[
CJK23
]
, the Earth Mover’s distance
[
AIK08
,
ABIW09
,
CJLW21
]
, the Euclidean minimum spanning tree
[
CCAJ+23
]
, facility location
[
CJK+22
]
, and clustering
[
BFL+17
]
. More broadly, the development of smaller coresets have also given rise significantly more efficient streaming algorithms for geometric problems (see, for example,
[
WY22
,
CW23
,
BCP+24
]
and references therein). For massively parallel computation, a line of work initiated by
[
ANOY14
]
on geometric problems has resulted in algorithms for Euclidean MST
[
JMNZ24
]
, Metric MST
[
ABJ+25
]
, Facility Location and Clustering
[
CGJ+24
]
, as well as probabilistic tree embeddings
[
AAH+23
]
.

The greedy algorithm of
[
MS08
]
was initially developed for dense max-cut, and found other applications in Gale-Berlekamp games
[
KS09
]
, and graph-based semi-supervised learning
[
WJC13
]
. In the context of sublinear algorithms,
[
Yar14
]
has also used random sampling to speed-up the execution of
[
MS08
]
for dense CSPs; the algorithm and analysis differs from this work (the algorithm is still sequential, and sampling done independently for each step).

2
Parallel and Subsampled Greedy Assignment

We will first analyze the following “parallel” and “subsampled” greedy assignment and phrase the main structural theorem. The analysis is based on the greedy algorithm of
[
MS08
]
with two important differences; we can both “parallelize” and “subsample” the assignment rule used in
[
MS08
]
while still controlling the quality of the cut. This will allow us to maintain all the necessary information needed to simulate the greedy algorithm in various models of sublinear algorithms. An important aspect of the subsequent theorem is being able to sample points from the metric with certain probabilities which are compatible with that metric (essentially, they will need to result in certain low-variance estimates, as in
[
dlVK01
]
). Toward that, we first define the following two definitions.

Definition 2.1

(Metric-Compatible Weights)
.

For a metric space
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
, we say that a set of weights
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})\in(0,1/2]^{n}
is
\lambda
-compatible if, for every
i,j\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{w_{j}}\leq\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{k}).

Definition 2.2

(Activation Timeline)
.

Given an end time
t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
and a weight
w\in(0,1/2]
, an activation timeline
\mathcal{T}(w)
is a distribution supported on strings
\{0,1\}^{t_{e}}
sampled according to the following procedure:

•

In order to generate
\boldsymbol{a}\sim\mathcal{T}(w)
, we iterate through all
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
.

•

For each
t
, we generate
\boldsymbol{a}_{t}\in\{0,1\}
by first setting

\boldsymbol{w}^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\min\{w,1/t\}&\forall\ell<t,\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell}=0\\
w&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

and then sampling
\boldsymbol{a}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}^{t})
.

For a draw
\boldsymbol{a}\sim\mathcal{T}(w)
, the smallest
\boldsymbol{t}
with
\boldsymbol{a}_{\boldsymbol{t}}=1
is denoted the activation time.

We will use Definitions
2.1
and
2.2
as a means of “parallelizing” the cut assignment rule. In particular, we will consider a metric
(X,d)
consisting of dataset points, and every point
x_{i}
in the metric
(X,d)
will be able to compute a metric-compatible weight
w_{i}
. Since
t_{e}
will be a fixed parameter,
x_{i}
can sample an activation timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
, and the activation timeline will determine the “time” it is assigned to either side of the cut—namely, this will be the activation time of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
. If
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is never set to
1
, then the assignment will be a default value, which we will show occurs with small enough probability so as to not affect the final quality of the cut. At the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
for
x_{i}
, it will determine the assignment by considering other points in the metric which had already been activated by time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
. The last ingredient for our algorithm will be the way to “subsample” the assignment rule so that, even though many points in the metric may be activated, the assignment rule depends only on a few of them.

Definition 2.3

(Masking)
.

For an end time
t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
, as well as a keeping time
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{N}
and a parameter
\gamma\geq 1
, the mask
\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
is a distribution supported on strings
\{0,1\}^{t_{e}}
which is sampled according to the following procedure:

•

In order to generate
\boldsymbol{k}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
, we iterate through all
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
.

•

For each
t
, we generate
\boldsymbol{k}_{t}\in\{0,1\}
by first setting

\gamma^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&t\leq t_{0}\\
\min\{\gamma/t,1\}&t>t_{0}\end{array}\right.,

and we sample
\boldsymbol{k}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
.

As sketched above, each point
x_{i}
will compute a
\lambda
-metric-compatible weight
w_{i}
and generate an activation timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
. In addition, each point will also sample a mask
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. We will incorporate the mask in the following way: a point
x_{i}
is assigned to a side of the cut at its activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
by considering other points in the metric which have been activated before time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
, and in addition, were also “kept” according to the mask. Namely, the assignment of
x_{i}
will depend on point
x_{j}
if there exists
\ell<\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1
. As a result, even though many points
x_{j}
will be activated by time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
, only a few will be activated and kept for the same time
\ell<\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
. There is one final component needed in order to describe the assignment rule.

Definition 2.4

(Seed)
.

For the
n
timelines
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
sampled from
\mathcal{T}(w_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{T}(w_{n})
, we let:

•

The seed length
\boldsymbol{m}\in\mathbbm{N}
be given by
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
, where
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left\{j\in[n]:\exists\ell\leq t_{0}\text{ where }\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}=1\right\}.

•

The seed
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
assigns the points
x_{j}
for
j\in\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
to the
0
-side or the
1
-side of the cut. It does this by:

–

Considering a lexicographic order
\pi
on the set of points
\{x_{j}:j\in\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}\}
,

–

Letting
x_{j}
be assigned to the
\sigma_{\pi(x_{j})}
-side of the cut.

2.1
The
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(\cdot,\cdot)
Sub-routine

As in
[
MS08
]
, we encode (partial) cuts by
n\times\{0,1\}
Boolean matrices
z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
, where the
i
-th row of the matrix will be
(0,0)
, in the case that point
i
is currently not assigned,
(1,0)
, in the case that it is assigned to the
0
-side of the cut, or
(0,1)
, in the case that it is assigned to the
1
-side of the cut. The
n
rows of a matrix
z
encoding a partial cut are naturally associated with the
n
points of the metric. Toward that end, we describe below an assignment procedure which, under our setup (specified below) and a small amount of additional information, can consider a point
x_{i}
and its activation time
t_{i}
, and output
z_{i}\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}
. The necessary global information is captured in the following, “timeline-mask” summary, which will maintain the necessary information from a collection of activation timelines and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
for
j\in[n]
needed to perform an assignment.

Definition 2.5

(Timeline-Mask Summary)
.

Consider a metric
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
, a set of weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in[0,1/2)
, as well as parameters
t_{e},t_{0}\in\mathbbm{N}
and
\gamma\geq 1
.

•

Sample activation timelines and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
where
\mathbf{A}_{j}\sim\mathcal{T}(w_{j})
and
\mathbf{K}_{j}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
for every
j\in[n]
.

•

The summary of
(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{1}),\dots,(\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K}_{n})
is given by the set of tuples

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}

\displaystyle=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j};j\in[n])

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left\{(x_{j},\ell;w_{j},t_{j}):\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1\text{ and }t_{j}\text{ is the activation time of $\mathbf{A}_{j}$}\right\}.

We now turn to describing the main online assignment sub-routine; this is the algorithm which decides given a point (as well as access to some additional information), which side of the cut the point lies in. We assume that there is an underlying metric
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
, and that the timelines and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{K}_{i})
for the metric have already been sampled, where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. The online assignment rule relies on the following “global information:”

•

A timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j}:j\in[n])
, as specified in Definition
2.5
, which summarizes information of the activation timelines and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{K}_{i})
succinctly. In particular, we will show that
|\mathbf{P}|
is small, and hence the summary
\mathbf{P}
may be easily stored and communicated.

•

A seed
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
for the draw
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
, as specified in Definition
2.4
. We note that the length of the seed,
\boldsymbol{m}
, is of size
|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
, corresponding to points
x_{i}
where
\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}=1
for some
\ell\leq t_{0}
. Since
\mathbf{K}_{i,\ell}=1
for all
\ell\leq t_{0}
, the points participating in
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
are stored in
\mathbf{P}
; so
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|\leq|\mathbf{P}|
, and
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
may be re-constructed from
\mathbf{P}
.

Assignment Rule

Assign
{}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
. The activation timelines and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
for all
j\in[n]
have been sampled, and
\mathbf{P}
is a timeline-mask summary for the activation timelines and masks, and that
\sigma
is a seed for
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
. Since the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
is completely determined by
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
for
j\in[n]
, we refer to the rule as
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
for convenience.

Input
: The point
x_{i}
to be assigned and the activation time
t_{i}
for the timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}
.

Inductive Hypothesis
: We assume the algorithm has access to a partial cut
\boldsymbol{z}=z(\sigma,\mathbf{P},t_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}
which assigns every point
x_{j}
which participates in a tuple of
\mathbf{P}
and whose activation time
t_{j}
is smaller than
t_{i}
.

Output
: The assignment, either
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
corresponding to the 0-side or 1-side of the cut, for the
i
-th point
x_{i}
.

•

Base Case
: If
t_{i}\leq t_{0}
, the point
x_{i}
has index
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
.
4
4

4

There is a minor ambiguity here as to whether the algorithm which receives the point
x_{i}
also knows the index
i
. On the first reading, it is helpful to consider an algorithm which knows the index. This makes it easier notationally to index assignments of a point
x_{i}
by the
i
-th entry of
\boldsymbol{z}
. However, we note that this is only for notational convenience, as we could store the partial cut
\boldsymbol{z}
as a function from a subset of the points
X
to
\{(1,0),(0,1)\}
. We keep the indices
i
, as this helps connect the assignment procedure to the analysis, however, in the algorithm any uses of the indices may be replaced by indexing according to the point.
Note that the set of points
\{x_{j}:j\in\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}\}
can be re-constructed from
\mathbf{P}
, and hence, the index
\pi(x_{i})\in[\boldsymbol{m}]
can be determined. Output
(1,0)
if
\sigma_{\pi(x_{i})}=0
and
(0,1)
if
\sigma_{\pi(x_{i})}=1
.

•

Incorporating Estimates
: Otherwise,
t_{i}>t_{0}
and we proceed as follows:

1.

As per Definition
2.2
for every
(x_{j},\ell;w_{j},t_{j})\in\mathbf{P}
, we let
\boldsymbol{w}^{\ell}_{j}=\min\{w_{j},1/\ell\}
if
\ell=t_{j}
and
w_{j}
if
\ell\geq t_{j}
. Furthermore, as per Definition
2.3
, we let
\gamma^{\ell}=\min\{\gamma/\ell,1\}
. We consider the subset of pairs

\mathbf{P}_{t_{i}}=\left\{(x_{j},\ell):(x_{j},\ell;w_{j},t_{j})\in\mathbf{P}\text{ and }\ell<t_{i}\right\}

which have been assigned by the inductive hypothesis (by recursively executing
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{j},t_{j})
).

2.

We compute the estimated contributions, from the
0
-side and
1
-side of the cut, at the time
t_{i}
:

\mathbf{C}_{0}=\sum_{(x_{j},\ell)\in\mathbf{P}_{t_{i}}}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}_{j,0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{C}_{1}=\sum_{(x_{j},\ell)\in\mathbf{P}_{t_{i}}}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}_{j,1}.

Output
(0,1)
if
\mathbf{C}_{0}>\mathbf{C}_{1}
(to assign
x_{i}
to the
1
-side of the cut), and
(1,0)
otherwise (to assign
x_{i}
to the
0
-side of the cut).

Figure 3:
The assignment procedure
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)

The assignment rule
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
appears in Figure
3
. It takes in a point
x_{i}
and its activation time
t_{i}
, as well as access to the seed
\sigma
and the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
. We note that the algorithm assumes, by inductive hypothesis, that all points
x_{j}
participating in tuples of
\mathbf{P}
with activation times before
t_{i}
have been assigned. This is done by a recursive application of
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{j},t_{j})
. Given a draw
(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{1})
, …,
(\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K}_{n})
, the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
is completely determined (and hence
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
is determined as well), but there are
2^{\boldsymbol{m}}
choices of
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
. Hence, we will oftentimes drop the notation
\mathbf{P}
from the assignment rule, and simply refer to
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

2.2
Main Structural Theorem

We may now state our main structural theorem, which shows that the cut obtained from executing the assignment rule
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
in parallel (for the best choice of
\sigma
) gives an approximately optimal max-cut for certain setting of parameters. Similarly to
[
MS08
]
, we will show that there exists a setting of
\sigma
for which
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
produces a cut
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
which approximately minimizes the sum of
internal distances

\displaystyle f(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(z_{i,0}z_{j,0}+z_{i,1}z_{j,1}\right),

(2)

up to an additive factor of
O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})
. Since every pair
(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]
is either on the same side of the cut, or across the cut, minimizing
f
corresponds to maximizing the sum of cut edges, up to the additive error of
O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})
.

Theorem 4
.

Fix an
n
-point metric
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
, a collection of weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in(0,1/2]
which is
\lambda
-compatible, and the setting of parameters

\displaystyle t_{e}\geq\frac{n\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon}\qquad\gamma\geq\frac{(\ln(t_{e})+1)^{2}\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad t_{0}\geq\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\cdot\lambda}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}.

Consider the following setup:

•

Draw
n\times t_{e}
matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{K}
, where the
i
-th rows
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
.

•

For any seed
\sigma
of
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
, let
\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma)
denote the following cut:

\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma)_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})&\boldsymbol{t}_{i}\text{ is the activation time for $\mathbf{A}_{i}$}\\
(1,0)&\text{$\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=0$ for all $t\in[t_{e}]$}\end{array}\right..

Then,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[\min_{\sigma}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))\right]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

We defer the proof of Theorem
4
to Section
5
, and show how to use the theorem in massively parallel computation and insertion-only streaming.

3
O(1)-Round Fully Scalable MPC Algorithm

In the massively parallel computation model (MPC), a cluster of machines is given as input a set of points
X\subset\mathbbm{R}^{d}
partitioned arbitrarily across all machines. Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
needs to compute an approximate max-cut assignment
z_{i}\in\{(0,1),(1,0)\}
for each point
x_{i}
in it’s partition
X_{j}
. The key constraint of the MPC model is the memory of each machine. We consider a fully scalable (also known as strongly sublinear) regime, where there are
m=\Theta(\frac{nd}{s})
machines which partitions the points, each with local memory
s=\Theta(n^{\alpha}d)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Note that the amount of communication per round between machines is also bounded by
s
; no machine can send or receive more than
s
words of communication.
5
5

5

A word contains
O(\log(nd\Delta/\varepsilon))
bits, where
\Delta
is the aspect ratio of the points.
We consider duplicate points to be separate and distinct points, which if desired can easily be removed in
O(1)
-rounds
[
Gha19
]
. We show how to apply the parallel and sparsified assignment rule to get an
O(1)
-round, fully-scalable algorithm for approximating the max-cut of
X\subset\mathbbm{R}^{d}
in any
\ell_{p}
norm, for
p\in[1,2]
.

Theorem 5
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
p\in[1,2]
, there exists a randomized fully scalable MPC algorithm with the following guarantees:

•

The algorithm receives as input a multi-set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
distributed across
m
machines each with local memory
s
of at least
dn^{\alpha}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
words for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
.

•

After
O_{\alpha}(1)
-rounds of communication, each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
can compute for all points
x_{i}\in X
in its memory, a (randomized) assignment
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}),

(3)

where the expectation is taken over the internal randomness of the algorithm, and
f
is in (
2
) with
\ell_{p}
-metric.

The total space used by the algorithm is
O(nd)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd)/\varepsilon)
.

Remark 6
.

When instantiating Theorem
5
to Euclidean instances (i.e.,
p=2
) one may apply an MPC implementation of the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma (Theorem 3 of
[
AAH+23
]
) which runs in constant rounds and is fully-scalable with total space
O(nd+n\log^{3}n/\varepsilon^{2})
. This means one may consider the above theorem with
d=O(\log n/\varepsilon^{2})
and obtain Theorem
5
with total space
O(nd)+n\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n)/\varepsilon)
.

At a high level, the algorithm consists of generating the global information necessary to execute
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
for the best seed
\sigma
(see Figure
3
) and apply Theorem
4
. This first involves each machines computing metric-compatible weights of its points (Definition
2.1
). This allows each machine to independently sample the activation timelines (Definition
2.2
) and masks (Definition
2.3
) for each of the points held in each machine, and to compute the set of tuples
\mathbf{P}
of the global information needed for
Assign
{}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Elements of
\mathbf{P}
are then shared across all machines, alongside the setting of the seed
\sigma
which the root of a converge-cast primitive can compute. Now each machine has access to the global information necessary to compute
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(\cdot,\cdot)
for all of it’s points. Before we prove Theorem
5
, we first outline the important primitives used as subroutines.

3.1
MPC Preliminaries

Our algorithm makes use of the following standard MPC primitives:

Lemma 3.1

(Lemma 2.2 in
[
GSZ11
]
)
.

There exists an
O(\log_{s}n)
-round deterministic sorting algorithm for
m=\Theta(\frac{n}{s})
machines each with
s=n^{\alpha}
words of memory for any
\alpha\in(0,1]
, such that on input
\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}=P
where
p_{1}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}
when the algorithm terminates, if machine
\mathcal{M}
holds element
p_{i}
, it knows the rank
i
.

Lemma 3.2

(Broadcast & Converge-cast
[
Gha19
]
)
.

Given a cluster of machines each with
s
words of memory, one can define a communication tree with a branching factor of
\sqrt{s}
, where each node is a machine. The tree has total depth
O(\log_{s}(n))
. Using this tree, the root machine
\mathcal{M}_{0}
can broadcast
\sqrt{s}
words to all machines in
O(\log_{s}(n))
rounds. Likewise the machines can communicate
\sqrt{s}
total words to the root machine
\mathcal{M}_{0}
in
O(\log_{s}(n))
rounds, provided each machine never receives more than
s
total words or sends more than
\sqrt{s}
words. These procedures are referred to as broadcast and converge-cast, respectively.

3.2
Computing Geometric Weights

The first main ingredient in our algorithm is a method to compute metric-compatible weights (Definition
2.1
). We use the
\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})
-matrix sketches from Theorem
19
in order to determine the weight. The algorithm is a direct application of the sketches with some additional bookkeeping.

Lemma 3.3

(Geometric weights in MPC)
.

For any
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and
p\in[1,2]
, there exists a randomized fully scalable MPC algorithm satisfying the following:

•

Given as input a multi-set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
distributed across machines
m
whose local memory
s
is at least
O(dn^{\alpha})\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)
for
\alpha\in(0,1]
.

•

After
O_{\alpha}(1)
rounds of communication, each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
computes and stores a weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\in[0,1]
for all points
x_{i}
in its memory.

With probability at least
1-\delta
over the internal randomness of the algorithm, every
i\in[n]
satisfies

\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{k},x_{j})}\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{k},x_{j})}.

MPC Algorithm

Compute-Weights
. The algorithm is used as a sub-routine within the MPC algorithm to compute metric-compatible weights.

Input
: The parameters
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and a multi-set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
distributed across machines each with local memory
s
which can fit, in addition to the points store,
dn^{\alpha}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)
words for
\alpha\in(0,1]
. The subset of
X
distributed to machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
is denoted
X_{j}
.

Output
: Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
which holds the set
X_{j}
can determine, for each point
x_{i}\in X_{j}
, the index
i\in[n]
and a weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}
.

1.

The machines jointly compute
n
, the total number of points (using Lemma
3.2
), as well as a rank (i.e., labels)
i\in[n]
for each point in
X
(using Lemma
3.1
). We assume, henceforth, that each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
which holds
x_{i}
in its memory “knows” the index
i\in[n]
.

2.

Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
creates an
n\times d
matrix
A^{j}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d}
such that the
i
-th row
A_{i}^{j}
is set to

\displaystyle A^{j}_{i}=\begin{cases}x_{i}&\ \text{if }x_{i}\in X_{j}\\
\smash{\underbrace{(0,0,\dots,0)}_{\text{$d$ times}}}&\ \text{o.w.}\end{cases},

and we let
A=\sum_{j=1}^{m}A^{j}
be the
n\times d
matrix which stacks points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbbm{R}^{d}
as the rows of the matrix.

3.

Using shared (or communicated) randomness, the machines draw a single
\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)\times nd
matrix
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
, from Theorem
19
with accuracy
\varepsilon/3
and failure probability
\delta/n
,
p
as received and
k=1
. Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
can independently compute
\smash{\mathbf{sk}(A^{j})=\mathbf{S}\cdot A^{j}}
, and by broadcasting and converge-cast, can compute
\mathbf{sk}(A)=\mathbf{S}\cdot A
.

4.

Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
iterates through each input
x_{i}\in X_{j}
and creates the
n\times d
matrix
x_{i}^{\circ}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d}
which places
x_{i}
on all
n
rows. Each machine executes the evaluation algorithm from Theorem
19
on
\mathbf{sk}(A)-\mathbf{sk}(x_{i}^{\circ})
to compute
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
. The algorithm broadcasts and converge-casts
\mathbf{D}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
.

5.

A machine which holds the point
x_{i}\in X_{j}
sets the weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}
to the minimum of
(1+\varepsilon/3)\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{i}/\mathbf{D}
and
1
.

Figure 4:
MPC Algorithm for Computing Geometric Weights

Proof:

We present the algorithm in Figure
4
, and we refer to elements of the algorithm in this proof. First, observe that that
\sum_{j=1}^{m}A^{j}=A
where
A\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d}
is the matrix of each
x_{i}
stacked together, such that
x_{i}
is the
i
-th row of
A
. Also, note that for all
i\in[n]
,
\|A-x_{i}^{\circ}\|_{\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
. Hence by the properties of Theorem
19
and a union bound, we have that for any
i\in[n]
, we compute a
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{j},x_{i})\leq\boldsymbol{d}_{i}\leq\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{j},x_{i}).

By summing over all
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
, we get an estimate
\mathbf{D}
which is a
(1+\varepsilon/3)
-approximation of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
. Then, letting
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}=\min\{(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{i}/\mathbf{D},1\}
, which by a simple computation satisfies the following property:

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{k},x_{j})}\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{k},x_{j})}.

All we now need is to bound the space and communication used. Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
store
n
, the ranks
i
for each
x_{i}\in X_{j}
in local memory. The matrices
A^{j}
only contain non-zero entries for the points stored in machine
\mathcal{M}_{i}
and hence fits into local memory. The shared randomness used to draw
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
from Theorem
19
, is bounded by
\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)
and hence also fits into local memory. Each
\mathbf{sk}(A^{j})
takes no more than
\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)
words to store. The partial sums also take at no more than
\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)
words to store, by the properties of the sketch. The sums
\mathbf{D}
and weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}
are stored in one word each. Each phase of communication (sorting, converge-cast, broadcast) each take
O(\log_{s}(n))
rounds for a total
O(\log_{s}(n))
rounds of communication.

3.3
Main MPC Algorithm and Proof of Theorem
5

The description of the main MPC Algorithm for Euclidean Max-Cut appears in Figure
5
. We will use the algorithm to prove Theorem
5
, whose proof is divided within the two subsequent subsections.

MPC Algorithm

E-Max-Cut
.

Input
: An accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, a multi-set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
distributed across machines with at least one non-equal point (otherwise, all cuts have value
0
). Each machine has local memory
s\geq\mathrm{poly}(d\log(n)/\varepsilon)
and there are
m=\Theta(n/s)
machines. For each
j\in[m]
, we denote the subset of
X
in machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
as
X_{j}
.

Output
: For each
j\in[m]
, the machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
can determine a (random) assignment
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}
for each
x_{i}\in X_{j}
.

1.

We utilize the sub-routine
Compute-Weights
so that each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
may compute a weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}
for each
x_{i}\in X_{j}
accuracy
\varepsilon
,
p
and failure probability
\delta\leftarrow\varepsilon
(see Lemma
3.3
).

2.

The machines determine the parameter
n=|X|
, and set parameters

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=8\qquad t_{e}=\frac{n\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon}\qquad\gamma=\frac{(\ln(t_{e})+1)^{2}\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\cdot\lambda}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\},\qquad\xi=\mathrm{poly}\left(\frac{\lambda\log(n)}{\varepsilon}\right)

so as to align with the parameter settings of Theorem
4
, Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
A.2
.
6
6

6

As we will see, the value of
\log|\mathcal{F}|
when applying Lemma
A.2
will be
\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
.
Using these parameter settings, each
\mathcal{M}_{j}
samples for each
x_{i}\in X_{j}
, an activation timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\prime})
where
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{w}_{i}/2
, a mask
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
, and a bit
\boldsymbol{c}_{i}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\prime},1\})
.

3.

For each
j\in[m]
, machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
computes

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\{(x_{i},\ell;\boldsymbol{w}_{i},t_{i}):x_{i}\in X_{j},\ell\in[t_{e}],\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{i,\ell}=1,t_{i}\text{ is activation time of $\mathbf{A}_{i}$}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\{(x_{i},\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{i},t_{i}):x_{i}\in X_{j},\boldsymbol{c}_{i}=1,t_{i}\text{ is activation time of $\mathbf{A}_{i}$}\right\}.

Machines converge-cast the union of
\mathbf{P}_{j}
to compute
\mathbf{P}
, and the union of
\mathbf{C}_{j}
to compute
\mathbf{C}
. The root machine of converge-cast uses Lemma
A.2
to decide on a seed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
. The root then broadcasts
\mathbf{P}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
.

4.

Each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
, using
\mathbf{P}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
can then compute
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
for all points
x_{i}\in X_{j}
.

Figure 5:
MPC Algorithm for Euclidean Max-Cut.

3.3.1
Correctness Guarantee of Theorem
5

We let
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}
denote the cut assignment produced by the MPC algorithm,
E-Max-Cut
, and we will use Theorem
4
to show (
3
) is satisfied. First, we apply Lemma
3.3
to conclude that, with probability
1-\varepsilon
, all weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\in(0,1]
computed in Line
1
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
A.1
with
\mathsf{D}=1+\varepsilon
, where weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}>0
since there are at least two non-equal points. Assume, from now on, that this is the case; with probability at most
\varepsilon
, the above fails and we will later bound the expected contribution from such “catastrophic” failures. By Lemma
A.1
, the weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{w}_{i}/2
are
8
-compatible and satisfy
\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}\|_{1}\leq(1+\varepsilon)/2
. We now apply Theorem
4
with the weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}^{\prime}\in(0,1/2]
with parameter settings according to Line
2
; each point
x_{i}
computes an activation timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\prime})
and masks
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. In Subsection
3.3.2
, we will show the expectation of
|\mathbf{P}|
is at most
\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
; with probability
1-\varepsilon
,
|\mathbf{P}|\leq\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
. Assume this is the case (otherwise, with probability
\varepsilon
, we obtain another “catastropic failure), and note this implies a seed for
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
has length at most
\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
.

After the converge-cast of
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{C}
in Line
3
, the root machine contains the necessary components for Lemma
A.2
(because
\log|\mathcal{F}|
is at most the seed length
\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
). In particular, the root machine has access to
\mathbf{P}
, so it can compute, for any seed
\sigma
, the value
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
for every
(x_{i},\xi\cdot w_{i},t_{i})\in\mathbf{C}
. This allows the algorithm to determine a setting of
\sigma^{*}
, which can be broadcasted, along with
\mathbf{P}
to every machine. In Line
4
, each machine
\mathcal{M}_{j}
now has all the global information needed to compute
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma^{*},\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
for all
x_{i}\in X^{j}
. In summary, the output vector
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}
satisfies, using
f
from (
2
) with
\ell_{p}
-metric,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K})}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})\right]

\displaystyle\mathop{\leq}^{\text{Lem.~\ref{lem:seed-check}}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K})}\left[\min_{\sigma}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))\right]+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})

\displaystyle\mathop{\leq}^{\text{Thm.~\ref{thm:main-structural}}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}).

We now upper bound the contribution of “catastrophic” failures, when the weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}
fail to satisfy the conditions to Lemma
3.3
, or when the seed length (which is bounded by
|\mathbf{P}|
) is more than
\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon)
. These cases occurs with probability at most
2\varepsilon
, and the maximum internal distance may occur when all inputs are assigned to the same side of the cut and contribute
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
. Hence, this incurs another factor of
\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
.

3.3.2
Round and Space Guarantees for Theorem
5

The total round complexity of this algorithm is
O(\log_{s}(n))
. It consists of the
Compute-Weights
subroutines of Line
1
(Lemma
3.3
), and the converge-casts and broadcasts of
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{C}
in Line
3
. It only remains to show that
|\mathbf{P}|
and
|\mathbf{C}|
is small; to see this, note that for a fixed setting of weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}
, the definition of
\mathbf{P}_{j}
(and hence
\mathbf{P}
) in Line
3
is

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K})}\left[|\mathbf{P}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t_{e}}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{i,\ell}=1\right]\leq(t_{0}+\gamma)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\boldsymbol{w}_{i}}{2}+\sum_{\ell=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\boldsymbol{w}_{i}}{2}\cdot\frac{\gamma}{t}

\displaystyle\leq O(t_{0}+\gamma)+O(\gamma\ln(t_{e}+1))\leq\mathrm{poly}(\log n/\varepsilon).

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{c}_{n}}\left[|\mathbf{C}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\boldsymbol{c}_{i}=1\right]\leq O(\xi).

Storing each entry in
\mathbf{P}
takes
O(d)
words for the point, the weight, time and activation time. Furthermore, each entry of
\mathbf{C}
similarly takes
O(d)
words. The size of the seed
\sigma
is bounded by
\mathbf{P}
. Since all of these values are smaller than
\sqrt{s}
words, the broadcast and converge-cast operations can be run in
O(\log_{s}(n))
rounds. There are a constant number of such subroutines, which each take the total round complexity is
O(\log_{s}(n))
, which is
O(1)
in the fully scalable regime.

4
Insertion-Only Streaming Algorithm

In this section, we show how to apply Theorem
4
to design an insertion-only streaming algorithm which makes one-pass over the data and provides query access to an approximately optimal max-cut in
\ell_{p}
space, for all
p\in[1,2]
. An insertion-only streaming algorithm is a small-space randomized algorithm which computes in the following manner:

•

Throughout the entire execution, the algorithm maintains a memory of
s
“words”, which contains enough bits to specify (i) indices for individual points, (ii) individual coordinates of points, and (iii) intermediate numbers involved in the computation. There will be
n
points encoded as vectors in
[\Delta]^{d}
, so a word will consist of
\Theta(\log(nd\Delta/\varepsilon))
bits. We assume that there are no duplicate points, so that
n
is always at most
[\Delta]^{d}
.

•

An input dataset
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
of points in
[\Delta]^{d}
is streamed to the algorithm one-at-a-time and in an arbitrary order. We will use indices
1,\dots,n
to specify the order the algorithm sees them, so that
x_{1}
is presented first, followed by
x_{2}
, …,
x_{n}
. Upon receiving each point
x_{i}\in X
, the algorithm may update its memory. Once the entire dataset has been inserted, the algorithm provides query access to a function which specifies, for each point
x_{i}\in X
, whether it belongs to the 0-side of a cut or the 1-side of a cut.

Remark 7

(Use and Storage of Randomness)
.

We present algorithms in the “random oracle model:” we assume the algorithm has query access to a function
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
which outputs, for any string in
\{0,1\}^{*}
, an independent and uniformly distributed random bit. The assumption is impractical, and it is fairly standard to use Nisan’s pseudorandom generator
[
Nis92
]
(see Section 4.3.3 in
[
Nel20
]
). In particular, the number of uniform random bits ever referenced is at most
\exp((d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)^{O(1)})
; furthermore, for any fixed set
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
, there is a
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
-space read-once branching program whose input are the
\exp((d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)^{O(1)})
random bits referenced and which computes the cost of the cut output. Hence, we let the algorithm use
\boldsymbol{r}
to (i) sample independent random variables (which will be used for Theorem
4
), and (ii) refer back to previously-used randomness without explicitly storing it.

Theorem 8
.

For any
p\in[1,2]
,
d,\Delta\in\mathbbm{N}
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists an insertion-only randomized streaming algorithm with the following guarantees:

•

Maintenance
: The streaming algorithm implicitly maintains a set of (distinct) points
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
, as well as a random seed
\boldsymbol{r}
, using
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
words of space.

•

Updates
: For a point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, the sub-routine
\textsc{Add-Point}(x)
updates the streaming algorithm so as to maintain
X\cup\{x\}
.

•

Query
: For a point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, the sub-routine
\textsc{Assign}(x)
outputs
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
, corresponding to the 0-side or 1-side of the cut.

For any set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
(indexed so that
x_{i}
is the
i
-th point inserted), consider executing
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
for all
i\in[n]
in order; once done, let
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
be

\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{Assign}(x_{i}).

Then,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})\right]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}),

using
f
from (
2
) with
\ell_{p}
-metric.

At a high level, the insertion-only streaming algorithm follows a pattern similar to the MPC algorithm. There is a sub-routine (a part of the streaming algorithm) which is dedicated to computing the weights using an
\ell_{1}(\ell_{k})
cascaded sketch, a sub-routine which maintains activation timelines and masks for individual points, and a sub-routine dedicated to selecting the final seed for the assignment procedure. Then, the streaming algorithm computes weights and maintains the points needed to execute the procedure of Theorem
4
. We incorporate a new component, a “cleanup” procedure, where the algorithm ensures that its space complexity will not exceed some bound by “forgetting” certain points.

4.1
Computing the Weights

We begin by specifying the streaming algorithm to compute metric-compatible weights. As in the MPC algorithm, we will also make use of the
\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})
-matrix sketches in order to determine the weight (see Lemma
19
). The algorithm is straight-forward, and uses an
\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})
sketch which are prepared in order to compute sum of all distances. As in Lemma
3.3
, rows of the matrix will be encoded with dataset points. We will also maintain a counter, which allows us to determine how many non-zero rows the matrix has.

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
p\in[1,2]
,
d,\Delta\in\mathbbm{N}
, and
\eta,\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists an insertion-only randomized streaming algorithm with the following guarantees:

•

Maintenance
: The streaming algorithm implicitly maintains a set of points
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
using
\mathrm{poly}(d\log(\Delta/\delta)/\eta)
words of space.

•

Updates
: For a point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, the sub-routine
\textsc{Add-Point}(x)
updates the streaming algorithm so as to maintain
X\cup\{x\}
.

•

Query
: For a point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, the sub-routine
\textsc{Weight}(x)
outputs a weight in
[0,1]
.

For any set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
and any
i\in[n]
, consider executing
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{j})
for all
j\leq i
in order. After inserting points
x_{1},\dots,x_{i}
, we let

\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})\leftarrow\textsc{Weight}(x_{j}),

for all
j\leq i
. Then, with probability
1-\delta
, every
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})\in[0,1]
satisfies

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\|x_{\ell}-x_{j}\|_{p}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\sum_{k=1}^{i}\|x_{\ell}-x_{k}\|_{p}}\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})\leq(1+\eta)\cdot\dfrac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\|x_{\ell}-x_{j}\|_{p}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\sum_{k=1}^{i}\|x_{\ell}-x_{k}\|_{p}},

where we default
0/0
as
1
(which occurs at
i=1
).

Streaming Algorithm

Compute-Weights
. The streaming algorithm is initialized with parameters
d,\Delta\in\mathbbm{N}
and
\eta,\delta\in(0,1)
and
p\in[1,2]
, and implicitly maintains a set of points
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
. At any moment, the algorithm may query a weight of a point
x
which we assume was previously inserted in
X
; the algorithm outputs a
(1+\eta)
-approximation to the sum of
\ell_{p}
-distances from each
x^{\prime}\in X
to
x
divided by the sum of all pairwise
\ell_{p}
-distances in
X
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Maintenance
. The streaming algorithm maintains the following information:

•

A counter
m
(initialized to zero), which counts the number of points inserted.

•

A sketch
\mathbf{sk}(A)
for the
\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})
norms of an implicit
\Delta^{d}\times d
matrix
A
(initially all zero), which contains the
m
inserted points as the first
m
rows (see Lemma
19
). The sketch is initialized by storing
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
from Lemma
19
with accuracy
\varepsilon\leftarrow\eta/3
and failure probability
\delta^{\prime}\leftarrow\delta/\Delta^{2d}
.

•

A total weight
\mathbf{W}
, initialized to
0
.

Update
.
Add-Point
(x)
.

1.

We increment the counter
m
.

2.

We update the sketch
\mathbf{sk}(A)
by adding the point
x
as the
m
-th row of the matrix. Notice this is done implicitly with the fact the sketch is linear: we consider the
\Delta^{d}\times d
matrix
A_{x}
which has
x
as the
m
-th row and is otherwise 0. The (implicit) matrix
A
is updated to
A+A_{x}
and the sketch updated to
\mathbf{sk}(A)+\mathbf{sk}(A_{x})
.

3.

We build the
\Delta^{d}\times d
matrix
x^{\circ}
which contains
x
as the first
m
rows, and we evaluate the answer on
\mathbf{sk}(A-x^{\circ})
the obtaining a number
\boldsymbol{d}
and update
\mathbf{W}\leftarrow\mathbf{W}+\boldsymbol{d}
.

Query
.
Weight
(x)
.

1.

Evaluate the sketch
\mathbf{sk}(A-x^{\circ})
(see above), to obtain a number
\boldsymbol{d}
. Note that this number may be different to the value
\boldsymbol{d}
obtained during
Add-Point
(x)
, since other points may have been inserted.

2.

Output
(1+\eta/3)\cdot\boldsymbol{d}/(2W)
if
W\neq 0
and the value is below
1
, and
1
otherwise.

Figure 6:
The
Compute-Weights
Streaming Algorithm.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
: Space Complexity.

The streaming algorithm
Compute-Weights
is described in Figure
6
, and it is clear that it uses
O(1)+\mathrm{poly}(d\log(\Delta/\delta)/\eta)
words of space: the counter
m
and total weight
W
are stored in one word each (note
W\leq n^{2}\Delta d
, which means it fits within a word), and the space complexity of the
\ell_{1}(\ell_{p})
-sketch comes directly from Lemma
19
for
\Delta^{d}\times d
matrices, with accuracy
\eta/3
and failure probability
\delta^{\prime}=\delta/\Delta^{2d}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
: Correctness.

We show correctness of the algorithm by maintaining the following invariant. Right after the execution of
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{1}),\dots,\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
,

•

The counter
m
is set to
i
.

•

The implicit matrix
A
consists of a
\Delta^{d}\times d
matrix whose first
i
rows are
x_{1},\dots,x_{i}
, and whose remaining rows are zero.

•

Let
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
denote the random variable output by Line
3
in the final execution of
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
over the randomness in
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
,

\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{i})\leq\boldsymbol{d}_{i}\leq(1+\eta/3)\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{i}).

•

For all
j\leq i
, let
\mathbf{W}_{j}
denote the random variable
\mathbf{W}
right after the execution of
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{j})
. Then, with probability at least
1-i\delta^{\prime}
over the randomness in initializing the sketch
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
, every
j\leq i
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})\leq\mathbf{W}_{j}\leq(1+\eta/3)\sum_{k=1}^{j}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k}).

(4)

We prove that the invariant holds by induction on
i
. For
i=1
, a single call of
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{1})
increments the counter once and updates the implicit matrix
A
in Line
2
to
A_{x_{1}}
, which is the matrix with a single non-zero first row set to
x_{1}
. The matrix
x_{1}^{\circ}
in Line
3
is also
A_{x_{1}}
, so by Lemma
19
,
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=0
with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
and, hence,
