\mathbf{W}_{j}=0
with probability
1-\delta^{\prime}
for all
j\leq i
. Suppose by induction that the invariant holds for index
i\in[n]
; we now execute
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i+1})
and verify the invariant for
i+1
. The counter increments from
i
to
i+1
and the implicit matrix
A
, adds the
i+1
-th row consisting of
x_{i+1}
to
A
. The random variable
\boldsymbol{d}_{i+1}
becomes the evaluation of the sketch
\mathbf{sk}(A-x_{i+1}^{\circ})
, which by Lemma
19
, satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{i+1}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{i+1})\leq\boldsymbol{d}_{i+1}\leq(1+\eta/3)\sum_{\ell=1}^{i+1}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{i+1})

(5)

with probability
1-\delta^{\prime}
. By a union bound over the invariant after
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
, with probability at least
1-(i+1)\delta^{\prime}
, the random variable
\boldsymbol{d}_{i+1}
during
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i+1})
satisfies (
5
) and
\mathbf{W}_{j}
satisfies (
4
) for all
j\leq i
. Thus, the updated value
\mathbf{W}_{i+1}
which is set to
\mathbf{W}_{i}+\boldsymbol{d}_{i+1}
after
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i+1})
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{i+1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{i+1}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{i+1})\leq\mathbf{W}_{i}+\boldsymbol{d}_{i+1}=\mathbf{W}_{i+1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq(1+\eta/3)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{i+1}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{i+1})\right)=(1+\eta/3)\sum_{k=1}^{i+1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k}).

This concludes the inductive proof that the invariant holds for all
i\in[n]
. Hence, after an execution of
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{1})
, …,
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
, the counter is
i
, the matrix
A
contains the points
x_{1},\dots,x_{i}
stacked as rows and remaining rows are zero, and the random variable
\mathbf{W}
stored in
Compute-Weights
is set to
\mathbf{W}_{i}
and satisfies (
4
) with
j=i
. We now execute
\textsc{Weight}(x_{j})
for all
j\leq i
and denote the random variable
\boldsymbol{d}_{j}^{\prime}
output by Line
1
in
\textsc{Weight}(x_{j})
. By Lemma
19
once again, each
\boldsymbol{d}_{j}^{\prime}
individually satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{j})\leq\boldsymbol{d}_{j}^{\prime}\leq(1+\eta/3)\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{j})

(6)

with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
over the randomness in
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
, so by a union bound,
\mathbf{W}_{i}
satisfies (
4
) and all
j\leq i
satisfy (
6
) with probability
1-2i\delta^{\prime}
. Notice that,
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})
is set to
(1+\eta/3)\boldsymbol{d}_{j}^{\prime}/(2\mathbf{W}_{i})
. The numerator
\boldsymbol{d}_{j}^{\prime}
satisfies (
6
) as desired, and
2\mathbf{W}_{i}
is a
(1+\eta/3)
-approximation to
\sum_{k=1}^{i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})
since
d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{\ell})=0
. Hence,
(1+\eta/3)\boldsymbol{d}_{j}/(2\mathbf{W}_{i})
is at least the desired quantity (which occurs when
\mathbf{W}_{i}
maximally over-estimates and is accounted by the additional
(1+\eta/3)
-factor), and is at most the desired quantity (which occurs when
\boldsymbol{d}_{j}^{\prime}
maximally over-estimates and obtains approximation
(1+\eta/3)^{2}\leq(1+\eta)
). By a union bound over all
i\in[n]
, all such
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})
satisfy the required guarantees with probability
1-2n^{2}\delta^{\prime}
. Since
n\leq\Delta^{d}
and
\delta^{\prime}=\delta/\Delta^{2\delta}
, we obtain the desired
1-\delta
.

4.2
Maintaining Activation and Kept Timelines

The second component is a streaming algorithm for maintaining an activation timeline and a mask for a particular point (recall Definition
2.2
and Definition
2.3
). The crucial aspect is that our streaming algorithm will only need to maintain information on points which were both activated and kept. As the streaming algorithm progresses and points enter the (implicit) set
X
, the metric-compatible weights change, and so do the set of points which are activated and kept.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
t_{0},t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
with
t_{0}\leq t_{e}
and
\gamma\geq 0
, there exists a data structure,
Timeline-Mask
(Figure
7
), which satisfies the following:

•

Initialization
: The algorithm is initialized by
\textsc{Init}(x,\boldsymbol{r})
, where
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
and
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
provides access to a random oracle.

•

Maintenance
: It stores a weight
w
(initialized to
1/2
) and implicitly maintains draws to an activation timeline
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{T}(w)
and a mask
\mathbf{K}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
.

•

Updates
: The weight may be updated with the sub-routine
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\tilde{w})
, which replaces
w\leftarrow\min\{w,\tilde{w}\}
. Furthermore, if
\mathbf{A}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
for all
t\in[t_{e}]
before the update, then after the update,
\mathbf{A}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}
is still
0
for all
t\in[t_{e}]
.

•

Query
: One can query
Activation-Time
()
, which returns the activation time of the timeline
\mathbf{A}
, and
Active-Kept
()
, which returns all indices
t\in[t_{e}]
where
\mathbf{A}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
.

The space complexity is that of storing the parameters
t_{0},t_{e},\gamma
,
w
, a pointer to
\boldsymbol{r}
, and the point
x
.

Remark 9

(Initialization Procedure
\textsc{Init}(x_{,}\boldsymbol{r})
)
.

We remark on an important aspect of the initialization above. Upon initialization for a point
x_{i}
with random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
, the data structure generates a timeline which only depends on
x_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{r}
and the weight
w_{i}
held. This has the following consequence. Suppose the algorithm receives as input the point
x_{i}
and initializes a
Timeline-Mask
data structure to maintain the activation timeline and mask
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{K}_{i})
. If there exists an index
t\in[t_{e}]
where
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{i,t}=1
, then point
x_{i}
is activated and kept. In the description of
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
in Figure
3
, the point
x_{i}
must be kept in the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
to assign other points
x_{j}
. If there is no time
t\in[t_{e}]
where
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{i,t}=1
, the algorithm may completely “forget” about
x_{i}
, since
x_{i}
is not used to assign other points. The benefit of forgetting is that it decreases the total amount of space when assigning points
x_{j}
. However, when assigning the point
x_{i}
, the assignment rule
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
needs to know
t_{i}
. Recall,
t_{i}
is the activation time of the timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}
, and we must argue that the algorithm may re-construct
t_{i}
. The guarantee that the timeline and mask depend solely on
x_{i},\boldsymbol{r}
and the weight
w_{i}
will allow us to re-create the activation timeline for
x_{i}
when generating the assignment for
x_{i}
.

Streaming Algorithm

Timeline-Mask
. The algorithm is initialized with parameters
t_{0},t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
and
\gamma>0
, and maintains a weight and implicitly an activation timeline
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{T}(w)
(Definition
2.2
) and a mask
\mathbf{K}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
(Definition
2.3
). The algorithm can support updates to weights (so long as they do not increase significantly), and can report the activation timeline and all indices
t
which were both activated and kept for that particular timeline and mask.

Maintenance
. The streaming algorithm is initialized with
\textsc{Init}(x,\boldsymbol{r})
, where
x
is a point and
\boldsymbol{r}
is a random oracle. It maintains the following information:

•

A weight
w
, initialized to
1/2
.

•

The point
x
, which will index to a starting position of the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
.

•

We use
\boldsymbol{r}
and
x
to generate a sequence of
2\cdot t_{e}
independent random variables

\mathbf{A}^{(r)}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}^{(r)}_{t_{e}}\sim[0,1]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{K}^{(r)}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{K}^{(r)}_{t_{e}}\sim[0,1].

•

The (implicit) activation timeline and mask are given by iterating through
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
, and letting

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bf 1}\left\{\mathbf{A}^{(r)}_{t}\leq\min\{w,\frac{1}{t}\}\right\}&\text{if }\forall\ell<t,\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(w)=0\\
{\bf 1}\left\{\mathbf{A}^{(r)}_{t}\leq w\right\}&\text{if }\exists\ell<t,\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(w)=1\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{t}

\displaystyle={\bf 1}\left\{\mathbf{K}^{(r)}_{t}\leq\gamma^{t}\right\},

(9)

where recall that
\gamma^{t}=1
if
t\leq t_{0}
and
\min\{\gamma/t,1\}
if
t>t_{0}
.

Update
.
Mod-Min-Weight
(\tilde{w})
.

1.

We update
w\leftarrow\min\{w,\tilde{w}\}
.

2.

If for all
t\in[t_{e}]
,
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
, the data structure reports “not activated and kept.”

Queries
.

•

For
\textsc{Activation-Time}()
, we find the smallest
t\in[t_{e}]
where
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)=1
and output
t
.

•

For
\textsc{Active-Kept}()
, we find all
t\in[t_{e}]
where
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
.

Figure 7:
The
Timeline-Mask
Data Structure.

Remark 10

(Using the Random Oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
and Finite Precision of Random Variables)
.

In Figure
7
, the data structure samples uniform random variables
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
for all
t\in[t_{e}]
in
[0,1]
, and it does this using the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
. The claim that random variables are “uniform in
[0,1]
” is an abuse in notation, since the algorithm can only maintain numbers up to a finite bit precision. Notice, however, that the random variables
\mathbf{A}^{(r)}_{t}
and
\mathbf{K}^{(r)}_{t}
are auxiliary, and the random variables affecting the execution of
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\tilde{w})
,
\textsc{Activation-Time}()
, and
\textsc{Active-Kept}()
are
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}
, which are thresholded values of
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
(see (
•
‣
7
) and (
9
)). Hence, it suffices to sample
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
up to the bit-precision of
\min\{w,1/t\}
(for the case of
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)
), and
\gamma^{t}
(for the case of
\mathbf{K}_{t}
). In particular, it suffices to use bit complexity
B
, which is
O(\log t_{e})
plus the largest bit complexity
w
and
\gamma^{t}
, in order to sample
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
. We now expand on how the algorithm uses
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
to sample these random variables in
\textsc{Init}(x,\boldsymbol{r})
. Given access to
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
and
x
:

•

The algorithm encodes the point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
as
\mathrm{Enc}(x)\in\{0,1\}^{d\log\Delta}
.

•

In order to sample the random variable
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
, it reads off each bit using
\boldsymbol{r}
. Namely, it considers the string
\tau
given by the concatenation of
\mathrm{Enc}(x)
,
\mathrm{Enc}(t)\in\{0,1\}^{\log t_{e}}
, “0” (specifying “
\mathbf{A}
”), and
\mathrm{Enc}(k)\in\{0,1\}^{\log B}
, and sets the
k
-th bit of
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
to
\boldsymbol{r}(\tau)
.

•

Similarly, the random variable
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
is also sampled by reading off each bit using
\boldsymbol{r}
. The string
\tau
now is given by the concatenation of
\mathrm{Enc}(x)
,
\mathrm{Enc}(t)\in\{0,1\}^{\log t_{e}}
, “1” (specifying “
\mathbf{K}
”), and
\mathrm{Enc}(k)\in\{0,1\}^{\log B}
, and sets the
k
-th bit of
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
to
\boldsymbol{r}(\tau)
.

Note the above upper bounds the number of uniform random bits referenced within
Timeline-Mask
data structures by
\exp((d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)^{O(1)})
, since we will set
t_{e}\leq O(\Delta^{d}/\varepsilon)
and
B
will be
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

We note that, since the weight is initialized to
1/2
and can only decrease (via the update
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\tilde{w})
), the weight is always between
0
and
1/2
. We will show that, for any point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
and any fixed weight
w
, the (implicit) random variables
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)
for
t\in[t_{e}]
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}
(as a function of the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
) are distributed as a timeline drawn from
\mathcal{T}(w)
, and a mask drawn from
\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. This is almost immediate—the fixed setting of
x
means that, over the randomness of
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
, the random variables
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
are independent and uniformly distributed in
[0,1]
. Hence, the variables
\mathbf{K}_{t}
are independent and distributed according to
\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
, as in Definition
2.3
. Similarly, the random variables
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)
are generated by iterating
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
,
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)
drawn by thresholding
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
with
\min\{w,1/t\}
, which is distributed as
\operatorname{Ber}(\min\{w,1/t\})
if
\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(w)=0
for all
\ell<t
, or by thresholding
\mathbf{A}_{t}^{(r)}
by
w
, which is distributed as
\operatorname{Ber}(w)
if
\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(w)=1
for some
\ell<t
. This procedure is exactly that which generates
\mathcal{T}(w)
in Definition
2.2
. Finally, whenever
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\tilde{w})
is executed, the weight
w
can only decrease, and hence
\mathbf{A}_{t}(\min\{w,\tilde{w}\})
(which would be the new activation timeline) will be below
\mathbf{A}_{t}(w)
.

4.3
Description of Insertion-Only Streaming Algorithm

We now show how to combine the components in Figure
6
and Figure
7
, as well as Lemma
A.2
, in order to execute the assignment rule from Theorem
4
. This will complete the description of the streaming algorithm and prove Theorem
8
. We first describe what the streaming algorithm maintains in its memory and the update procedure
Add-Point
(x)
. These definitions appear in Figure
8
. Then, we show how to implement the procedure
Assign
(x)
after performing a preprocessing step,
\textsc{Preprocess}()
.

Maintenance and Updates for

IOS-E-Max-Cut
. The algorithm is initialized with parameters
\Delta,d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
p\in[1,2]
, and an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. We describe the maintenance and update procedure
Add-Point
(\cdot)
, where for a set of points
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
presented as a stream (where necessarily
n\leq\Delta^{d}
), the stream is processed by executing the sub-routine
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
for every
i\in[n]
.

Maintenance
. The streaming algorithm initializes and stores parameters

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=60\qquad t_{e}=\frac{\Delta^{d}\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon}\qquad\gamma=\frac{(\ln(t_{e})+1)^{2}\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\cdot\lambda}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}\qquad\xi=\mathrm{poly}(t_{0}/\varepsilon\cdot d\log\Delta),

a random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
, and maintains the following information:

•

An instance of the
Compute-Weights
streaming algorithm from Figure
6
, initialized with
d,\Delta
, parameter
p
,
\eta=1
and
\delta=\varepsilon
.

•

A (random) set
\mathbf{C}
which is initially empty, and will consist of tuples of the form
(x,w)
, where
x
is a point in
[\Delta]^{d}
and
w\in(0,1/2]
.

•

A (random) set
\mathbf{T}
of instances of
Timeline-Mask
data structures from Figure
7
, initialized by
\textsc{Init}(x,\boldsymbol{r})
where
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, with
t_{e},t_{0}
and
\gamma
. The data structures in
\mathbf{T}
are indexed by points
x
; given
x
, we can retrieve its data structure in
\mathbf{T}
if it exists.

Update
.
Add-Point
(x)
.

1.

Execute the
Add-Point
(x)
update for the
Compute-Weights
streaming algorithm, and execute
Weight
(x)
and let
\boldsymbol{w}
denote the output and
\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{w}/2
.

2.

Sample a Bernoulli random variable
\mathbf{Z}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime},1\})
. If
\mathbf{Z}=1
, let
\mathbf{C}\leftarrow\mathbf{C}\cup\{(x,\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})\}
.

3.

Initialize an instance of
Timeline-Mask
with
\textsc{Init}(x,\boldsymbol{r})
and update
Mod-Min-Weight
(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})
. If the modified weight produced an output “not activated and kept,” delete the data structure. Otherwise, store the data structure in
\mathbf{T}
, indexed by the point
x
.

4.

Execute the following “clean-up” procedure:

(a)

For every tuple
(y,\sigma)\in\mathbf{C}
except possibly
(x,\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})
from Line
2
, execute
Weight
(y)
in
Compute-Weights
and let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
be the output divided by two. Sample
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{Ber}\left(\min\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}/\sigma,1\}\right)
; if
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}=1
, update
(y,\sigma)
to
(y,\min\{\sigma,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\})\in\mathbf{C}
, and if
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}=0
, delete
(y,\sigma)
from
\mathbf{C}
.

(b)

For every
Timeline-Mask
data structure in
\mathbf{T}
for point
y
except possibly
x
, execute
Weight
(y)
in
Compute-Weights
and let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
be the output divided by two. Execute
Mod-Min-Weight
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})
for the
Timeline-Mask
data structure and delete if it outputs “not activated and kept.”

Figure 8:
The maintenance and update procedure for
IOS-E-Max-Cut
.

Proof of Theorem
8
: Invariants of the Maintenance and Update.

We now show some important invariants that are satisfied by our maintenance and update procedure from Figure
8
when we initialize and add points to
IOS-E-Max-Cut
. These invariants will be used in the preprocessing step, as well as in the
\textsc{Assign}(\cdot)
sub-routine to connect with Theorem
4
. Specifically, we consider the following process:

•

First, initialize an instance of
IOS-E-Max-Cut
with the parameters
\Delta,d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
p\in[1,2]
, and accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

•

For a set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
, we execute
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{1})
,
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{2})
, …,
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{n})
.

Note that, in each execution of
Add-Point
(x_{i})
of
IOS-E-Max-Cut
, Line
1
executes
Add-Point
(x_{i})
in a
Compute-Weights
streaming algorithm initialized with
\eta=1
and
\delta=\varepsilon
(the parameters
d,\Delta
and
p
are passed from the initialization). We apply Lemma
4.1
directly to obtain the following guarantee: with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
over the randomness in
Compute-Weights
, every random variable
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})\in[0,1]
given by the output of
\textsc{Weight}(x_{j})
after inserting points
x_{1},\dots,x_{i}
(for
j\leq i
) satisfies

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{j})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\sum_{k=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})}\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})\leq 2\cdot\dfrac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{j})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\sum_{k=1}^{i}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{k})},

(10)

and
\boldsymbol{w}_{1}(x_{1})=1
(as expression (
10
) would be
0/0
). We will assume throughout the remainder of the analysis the above event occurs, and henceforth assume (
10
). The cases in which the above does not occur is considered a “catastrophic” failure; this occurs with probability at most
\varepsilon
. We will then similarly upper bound the effect of these failures as in the proof of Theorem
5
. We define the random variables, for every
i\in[n]
and
j\leq i
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})=\min\left\{\boldsymbol{w}_{\ell}(x_{j}):j\leq\ell\leq i\right\}.

(11)

Note that
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})
is always at most
1
(from the fact
\boldsymbol{w}_{\ell}(x_{j})\leq 1
) and they are greater than
0
since points are distinct.
Furthermore, we consider (solely for the analysis) keeping track of
n
many
Timeline-Mask
data structures,
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{n}
, where each data structure will correspond to a point
x_{i}
and is initialized with

\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{Init}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{r}).

As the calls for
Add-Point
(x_{1})
, …,
Add-Point
(x_{i})
execute, the data structures
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{n}
will be the ones initialized in Line
3
. Whenever Line
3
and Line
4b
perform an update to a
Timeline-Mask
data structure for
x_{i}
via
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\cdot)
, we update the corresponding data structures
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{i}
as well. We apply Lemma
4.2
directly, and conclude that for each
i\in[n]
, if
w_{i}
is the weight held in the data structure
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{i}
, it maintains a draw to an activation timeline and mask
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{K}_{i})
where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
with the weight stored in
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{i}
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. This way, the set of data structures
\mathbf{T}
is always a subset of
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{n}
.

Claim 4.3
.

For any
i\in[n]
, right after the execution of
Add-Point
(x_{1})
, …,
Add-Point
(x_{i})
, for any fixing of the randomness in
Compute-Weights
, we satisfy:

•

The set
\mathbf{C}
is a random subset of tuples, for every
j\leq i
,
(x_{j},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2)
is included in
\mathbf{C}
independently and with probability
\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2,1\}
, and randomness is over the samples in Line
2
and Line
4a
of
Add-Point
(x_{1})
, …,
Add-Point
(x_{i})
.

•

For
j\leq i
, the
Timeline-Mask
data structure
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
for point
x_{j}
has weight
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2
, and
\mathbf{T}
contains the data structure
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
if and only if the corresponding timeline and mask
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
, where
\mathbf{A}_{j}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2)
and
\mathbf{K}_{j}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
, have some
t\in[t_{e}]
with
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,t}=1
.

Proof:

We prove the above claim by induction on
i
. For
i=1
, only
Add-Point
(x_{i})
is executed;
\boldsymbol{w}
and
\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}
in Line
1
are set to
\boldsymbol{w}_{1}(x_{1})=1
and
1/2
, respectively. Hence,
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}(x_{1})=\boldsymbol{w}_{1}(x_{1})
. Line
2
samples
\mathbf{Z}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}(x_{1})/2,1\})
, and hence,
\mathbf{C}
contains
(x_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}(x_{1})/2)
independently with probability
\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}(x_{1})/2,1\}
right after Line
2
and Line
4a
does not modify it. For the second item, Line
3
initializes the data structure
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1}
with
\textsc{Init}(x_{1},\boldsymbol{r})
and sets the weight to
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}(x_{1})/2
. If
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1}
holds the activation timeline and mask
(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{1})
where
\mathbf{A}_{1,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{1,t}=0
for all
t\in[t_{e}]
, the data structure, Line
3
deletes it, and Line
4b
does not modify it. Hence,
\mathbf{T}
contains
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1}
if and only if there exists
t\in[t_{e}]
where
\mathbf{A}_{1,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{1,t}=1
.

We assume, for inductive hypothesis that Claim
4.3
holds for
i
, and we execute
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i+1})
. Similarly to above, the set
\mathbf{C}
contains the tuple
(x_{i+1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{i+1})/2)
independently with probability
\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{i+1})/2,1\}
(from Line
2
);
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{i+1}
is initialized in Line
3
and has weight
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{i+1})/2
and is stored in
\mathbf{T}
if and only if
\mathbf{A}_{i+1,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{i+1,t}=1
for some
t\in[t_{e}]
. It remains to check the condition for
j\leq i
. For the first item, the inductive claim is that right after the execution of
Add-Point
(x_{i})
(thus, right before
Add-Point
(x_{i+1})
),
(x_{j},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2)
is included in
\mathbf{C}
independently with probability
\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2,1\}
. Upon executing
Add-Point
(x_{i+1})
,

•

Line
4a
will eventually consider the tuple
(x_{j},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2)
. When it does, notice
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
becomes
\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j})/2
, and also
\min\{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j}),\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j})\}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{j})
. Hence,
(x_{j},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{j})/2)
is in
\mathbf{C}
independently with probability

\displaystyle\underbrace{\min\left\{\frac{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})}{2},1\right\}}_{\text{induction}}\times\underbrace{\min\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j})}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})},1\right\}}_{\text{case $\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}=1$}}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{\xi\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j})}{2},\frac{\xi\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})}{2},1\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{j})}{2},1\right\}

•

Line
4b
may consider the point
x_{j}
if
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
is in
\mathbf{T}
. Suppose
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
is not in
\mathbf{T}
; by induction, this is because
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
(which had weight
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})
) held an activation timeline and mask
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,t}=0
for all
t\in[t_{e}]
. For the analysis’s sake, we update
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
with
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j}))
, so
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,t}
is still
0
for all
t\in[t_{e}]
(by Lemma
4.2
). If
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
is in
\mathbf{T}
, then
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{j}
is eventually processed by Line
4b
and the weight updated via
Mod-Min-Weight
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})
where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j})/2
. Hence, the new weight becomes
\min\{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})/2,\boldsymbol{w}_{i+1}(x_{j})/2\}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i+1}(x_{j})/2
.

Proof of Theorem
8
:
Preprocess
()
.

We present a “preprocessing” sub-routine
Preprocess
()
in Figure
9
. The sub-routine is used to prepare the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
, as well as find the best seed in Figure
3
. As discussed for Claim
4.3
, we consider a fixed setting of
Compute-Weights
whose weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})
satisfy (
10
); recall, also, the definition of
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})
as the minimum of
\boldsymbol{w}_{\ell}(x_{j})
for
j\leq\ell\leq i
. The
n

Timeline-Mask
data structures
\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\textsf{DS}}_{n}
hold draws of the activation timeline and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{K}_{i})
, where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{i})/2)
and
\mathbf{K}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. The first step of
\textsc{Preprocess}()
(Line
1
in Figure
9
), is making the weights of time
Timeline-Mask
for
x_{j}
be
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30
, we do this by executing
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30)
, which sets the new weight to
\min\{\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})/2\}
. The following claim guarantees that the minimum of the two weights is
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30
, so we can assume henceforth, that the weight of
Timeline-Mask
for
x_{j}
is
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30
after Line
1
.

Claim 4.4
.

Suppose all
j\leq i
have weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})
satisfying (
10
). Then, for all
j\in[n]
,
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})\leq 15\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})
.

Proof:

Let
j\leq h<n
be that which satisfies
\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})
(if
h=n
, then we are automatically done), and consider
\boldsymbol{c}\sim\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
. We first expand using the lower bound on (
10
) and the triangle inequality to
\boldsymbol{c}
in order to say

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell})\right)+\sum_{\ell=h+1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\left[d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},\boldsymbol{c})\right]

(12)

\displaystyle\qquad+(n-h-1)\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\left[d_{\ell_{p}}(\boldsymbol{c},x_{j})\right].

We simply the right-hand side of (
12
) using the following two facts,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=h+1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\left[d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},\boldsymbol{c})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell}),

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\left[d_{\ell_{p}}(\boldsymbol{c},x_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\left[\dfrac{d_{\ell_{p}}(\boldsymbol{c},x_{j})}{\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})}\right]\leq 4\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{c}}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}d_{\ell_{p}}(\boldsymbol{c},x_{\ell})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell}),

where the second inequality applies Lemma
A.1
with the weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{1}),\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{h})
and the point
\boldsymbol{c}
. Thus, we simplify (
12
) by

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\ell},x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell})+\frac{4(n-h-1)}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq\left(5\cdot\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})+\frac{1}{n}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{\ell}),

which implies
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})\leq 10\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})+2/n
using (
10
). Finally, applying Lemma
A.1
for
\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{1}),\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{h})
, we have
\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})\geq 1/(2h)
and
h\leq n
,
10\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})+2/n\leq 15\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})-5/(2h)+2/n\leq 15\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})
.

For the point
x_{i}
, we let
t_{i}
be its activation time in
\mathbf{A}_{i}
. We first state and prove the following lemma, which will help us apply Lemma
A.2
(and later, Theorem
4
) by showing that the sampling weights guaranteed from Claim
4.3
are metric compatible.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that, for
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, the weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})
satisfy (
10
) for all
j\leq i
. Then, the weights
w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{n}^{\prime}
given by
w_{j}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})/2
from (
11
) are
8
-compatible, and the weights
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{1})/30,\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{n})/30
are
60
-compatible.

Proof:

Fix a setting of
j\in[n]
and suppose that
h\geq j
is that which minimizes
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})=\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})
. We show that Definition
2.1
holds by applying Lemma
A.1
with the points
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{h}\}
, the weight
w_{j}=\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})
, and the point
c=x_{i}
. Letting
w_{j}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{w}_{h}(x_{j})/2
, Lemma
A.1
implies

\displaystyle\dfrac{d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{w_{j}^{\prime}}\leq 8\sum_{k=1}^{h}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{k}),

and we use the fact distances are non-negative to conclude
\sum_{k=1}^{h}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{k})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
. The claim that
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{1})/30,\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{n})/30
are
60
-compatible by Lemma
A.1
as well.

Claim 4.6
.

After executing
Preprocess
()
from Figure
9
, the memory contents of
IOS-E-Max-Cut
satisfy the following guarantees:

•

The set
\mathbf{P}
is the timeline-mask summary
\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j}:j\in[n])
where
\mathbf{A}_{j}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30)
and
\mathbf{K}_{j}\cdot\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
(see Definition
2.5
).

•

Let
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
, and consider the family of complete cuts
z(\sigma)\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}
which are parametrized by
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
given by
z(\sigma)_{i}=\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
. Then, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

f(z(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\leq\min_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{m}}f(z(\sigma))+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}).

Proof:

We refer to Figure
9
and apply the guarantees of Claim
4.3
with
i=n
, as well as Lemma
A.2
. Note that, from Claim
4.3
and Claim
4.4
, the
Timeline-Mask
data structures in
\mathbf{T}
provide access to
(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j})
with
\mathbf{A}_{j}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{w}(x_{j})/30)
and
\mathbf{K}_{j}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
with
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,t}=1
for some
t\in[t_{e}]
, and Line
1
assembles these into
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j}:j\in[n])
. Recall that
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
consists of all points
x_{j}
whose activation time
t_{j}
in
\mathbf{A}_{j}
occurs before
t_{0}
; since
\mathbf{K}_{j,t_{j}}=1
if
t_{j}\leq t_{0}
, every
x_{j}\in\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
must is included in
\mathbf{P}
, and the algorithm may recover
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
. Thus, for fixed
\mathbf{P}
, the algorithm
Assign
{}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
defines an implicit family
\mathcal{F}
parameterized by
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
. Furthermore, the expectation of
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
is at most
O(t_{0})
since it consists of the points which are activated by time
t_{0}
, and we have
\|\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\|_{1}=O(1)
, and by Markov’s inequality, it is at most
O(t_{0}/\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
. If that is the case, then
\log|\mathcal{F}|=\boldsymbol{m}\leq O(t_{0}/\varepsilon)
, which is why we set
\xi=\mathrm{poly}(t_{0}/\varepsilon\cdot d\log\Delta).

We verify that we can apply Lemma
A.2
. From Claim
4.3
,
\mathbf{C}
is a random set which contains tuple
(x_{j},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})/2)
independently with probability
\min\{\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{i})/2,1\}
. Since the algorithm stores
(x_{j},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})/2)\in\mathbf{C}
and
\xi
, it knows the sampling probability
\min\{\xi\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{j})/2,1\}
. Finally, we note the algorithm may execute
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
for each
x_{i}
which participates in a tuple
(x_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{i})/2)\in\mathbf{C}
. The reason is that it may recover the activation time
t_{i}
(as discussed in Remark
9
); it computes
\textsc{Weight}(x_{i})
to recover
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{i})
, initializes the
Timeline-Mask
data structure with
\textsc{Init}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{r})
, executes
Mod-Min-Weight
(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{i})/2)
, and then sets
t_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{Activation-Time}()
. By setting the
\lambda
in Lemma
A.2
to
8
the weights
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{1}),\dots,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{n}(x_{n})
are
\lambda
-compatible (by Lemma
4.5
), the family
\mathcal{F}
has size
\log|\mathcal{F}|\leq O(t_{0}/\varepsilon)
, so
\xi
in Figure
8
is set accordingly as
\log n\leq d\log\Delta
, Lemma
A.2
implies
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
satisfies the approximately optimal condition.

Preprocess After Insertions
. We assume that for a set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
, the sub-routine
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{i})
has been executed for every
i\in[n]
(see Figure
8
). The
Preprocess
()
sub-routine prepares the following memory contents:

•

The set
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{j},\mathbf{K}_{j}:j\in[n])
, as per Definition
2.5
.

•

A seed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
for activation timelines
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
, as per Definition
2.4
.

Preprocess
()
.

1.

Initialize
\mathbf{P}=\emptyset
. Each
Timeline-Mask
for
x_{j}
in
\mathbf{T}
, stores the point
x_{j}
and executes
\textsc{Weight}(x_{j})
to obtain
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})
. We execute
\textsc{Mod-Min-Weight}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30)
for
x_{j}
’s timeline in order to make the final weight
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{j})/30
(from Claim
4.3
and Claim
4.4
).

2.

The
Timeline-Mask
for
x_{j}
in
\mathbf{T}
then executes
\textsc{Activation-Time}()
to obtain
t_{j}
, and
\textsc{Active-Kept}()
, letting
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}
be the output, update for each
\kappa\in[k]
,
\mathbf{P}\leftarrow\mathbf{P}\cup\left\{(x_{j},\ell_{\kappa};w_{j},t_{j})\right\}
.

3.

Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the family of complete cuts
z(\sigma)\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}
(referenced implicitly and parametrized by
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
) given by
z(\sigma)_{i}=\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
where
t_{i}
is the activation time of
x_{i}
, and the set
\mathbf{P}
is used as the timeline-mask summary (see Figure
3
).

4.

Execute the algorithm from Lemma
A.2
with accuracy
\varepsilon
and failure probability
\varepsilon
to select
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
, where we use
\mathbf{C}
for the random set (note that the activation time
t_{i}
for
x_{i}
can be recovered since
(x_{i},w_{i})\in\mathbf{C}
, see Remark
9
).

Figure 9:
Preprocessing for the Insertion-only Algorithm.

Proof of Theorem
8
: Space Complexity.

Notice from the maintenance procedure in Figure
8
and preprocessing procedure
9
, the streaming algorithm maintains:

•

A pointer to the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
, the parameters
\lambda,t_{e},\gamma,t_{0}
and
\xi
. Assuming that
\boldsymbol{r}
can be stored in small space (as we will use Nisan’s pseudorandom generator), the parameters use
O(d)
words of space.

•

A
Compute-Weights
streaming algorithm initialized with
d,\Delta
, and
p,\eta=1
and
\delta=\varepsilon
, using
\mathrm{poly}(d\log(\Delta/\varepsilon))
words of space (Lemma
4.1
).

•

The set
\mathbf{C}
uses
O(d)
words per element. After executing
Add-Point
(x_{1})
, …,
Add-Point
(x_{i})
,
|\mathbf{C}|
is a sum of
i
independent random variables in
[0,1]
whose expectation is
O(\xi)
. By Hoeffding’s inequality,
|\mathbf{C}|\leq O(d\xi\log\Delta)
with probability
1-\Delta^{-2d}
.

•

The set
\mathbf{T}
of
Timeline-Mask
data structures each use
O(d)
words of memory. After executing
Add-Point
(x_{1})
, …,
Add-Point
(x_{i})
,
|\mathbf{T}|
is at most the number of pairs
(j,\ell)\in[i]\times[t_{e}]
such that the activation timelines and masks
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1
, and furthermore, by Claim
4.3
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t_{e}}\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}\right]\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{t_{e}}\frac{\gamma}{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})}{2},

and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})\leq\boldsymbol{w}_{i}(x_{j})
, so
\sum_{j=1}^{i}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(x_{j})\leq 1
, giving us the bound
O(\gamma\log t_{e})
and simplifies to
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
. By Hoeffding’s inequality once more,
|\mathbf{T}|
is at most
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
with probability
1-\Delta^{-2d}
.

Finally, the summary set
\mathbf{P}
has space complexity dominated by that of
\mathbf{T}
, and the seed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
uses
m
bits, where
|m|\leq|\mathbf{P}|
. We may now take a union bound over at most
\Delta^{d}
steps of the algorithm and conclude that the space complexity does not exceed
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
with high probability throughout the entire execution.

Proof of Theorem
8
: Query and Output Guarantees.

The query
Assign
(\cdot)
for
IOS-E-Max-Cut
is described in Figure
10
. We will show that, after executing
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{1})
, …,
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{n})
, we may run
\textsc{Preprocess}()
to assemble the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
and the approximately optimal seed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
. For the final quality of the cut (and hence the proof of Theorem
8
), we apply Theorem
4
. We let
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
with
\ell_{p}
metric, and we let
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
be
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{1})/30,\dots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{n})/30\in(0,1/2]
, which are
60
-compatible by Lemma
4.5
whenever (
10
) holds, which occurs with probability
1-\varepsilon
over the randomness in
Compute-Weights
(see also Lemma
4.1
). Since
n
is at most
\Delta^{d}
, the parameter settings
t_{e},\gamma
and
t_{0}
are according to those in Figure
8
, the parameters satisfy the guarantees of Theorem
4
.

By Claim
4.3
, Claim
4.4
, and Claim
4.6
, the set
\mathbf{P}
is a timeline-mask summary for the activation timelines and masks
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{K}_{i})
, where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x_{i})/30)
and
\mathbf{K}_{i}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. Thus, any seed
\sigma
for
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
gives rise to the complete cut
\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma)
specified from
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
, and the algorithm
Assign
(x_{i})
provides query access to
\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
. We apply the second item in Claim
4.6
to say that except with probability
1-\varepsilon
over the randomness in
\mathbf{C}
, we satisfy the guarantees
f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))
is approximately optimal; so by a union bound over the randomness in
Compute-Weights
and the random set
\mathbf{C}
, we have

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\leq\min_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{m}}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})

with probability at least
1-2\varepsilon
. We take expectations over
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{K}
and applying Theorem
4
, so, except with probability
1-2\varepsilon
over randomness in
Compute-Weights
and
\mathbf{C}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\right]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+2\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}).

Finally, note that the maximum contribution that a cut may have is always at most
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
, so when we take expectation of the cut quality (even after averaging over randomness in
Compute-Weights
and
\mathbf{C}
), we satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\right]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+4\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}),

which concludes Theorem
8
.

Query Algorithm for
IOS-E-Max-Cut
. We assume that the sub-routines
Add-Point
(x_{1})
, …,
\textsc{Add-Point}(x_{n})
have been executed, and
Preprocess
()
has been run to store the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
and seed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
in memory. We specify the
Assign
(x)
sub-routine, which receives as input a point
x
, promised to belong to
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, and outputs
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
, corresponding to whether the point is assigned to the 0-side or 1-side of the cut.

Query
.
Assign
(x)
.

1.

First, we obtain the activation time for
x
with the following procedure:

•

If there is a
Timeline-Mask
data structure for point
x
in
\mathbf{T}
, then query the data structure with
Activation-Time
()
to obtain the activation time
t
.

•

If there is not: query
w\leftarrow\textsc{Weight}(x)
in
Compute-Weights
(which is set to
\boldsymbol{w}_{n}(x)
), initialize a
Timeline-Mask
with
\textsc{Init}(x,\boldsymbol{r})
and run
Mod-Min-Weight
(w/30)
; then, let
t\leftarrow\textsc{Activation-Time}()
(see Remark
9
).

2.

Since
\mathbf{P}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
have been stored by
\textsc{Preproces}()
, we execute
Assign
{}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}(x,t)
, and output whatever it outputs.
