Query for the Insertion-only Algorithm.

5
Proof of Theorem
4

To prove Theorem
4
, we describe a randomized algorithm, which receives as input access to the metric space, certain parameters which describe the random process, and (perhaps counter-intuitively,) a description of a minimizing cut
z^{*}
of the
f
. The random process then produces a (random) partial cut
\boldsymbol{z}
, as well as a string
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
which (i) will be short, and (ii) will encode the necessary information needed to simulate the process without access to
z^{*}
. We will then show that the cut
\boldsymbol{z}
which the process outputs is exactly the cut which the assignment rule
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}(\cdot,\cdot)
(in Figure
3
) can recreate (without knowing
z^{*}
).

Process.

We call the random process
GreedyProcess
, which we describe as a randomized algorithm with the following input/output behavior:

•

Input
: access to the metric space
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
, a vector
w\in(0,1/2]^{n}
encoding weights, times
t_{0},t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
, a parameter
\gamma\geq 1
, and a matrix
z^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
which will be a minimizer of
f
encoding cuts:

z^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}\left\{f(z):z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\text{ where every $i\in[n]$ has $z_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}$}\right\}.

•

Output
: the process outputs a random (partial) cut
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
where
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1),(0,0)\}
for all
i\in[n]
. In addition, a number
\boldsymbol{m}\in\mathbbm{N}
, and a string
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
.

The algorithm is described below.

Description.

GreedyProcess
((X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d),w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e},z^{*})
proceeds in rounds
t=1,2,\dots,t_{e}
, and will
generate the following sequences of random variables:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{t}\text{: a matrix $\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}$ which encodes a partial cut at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{t}\text{: a vector of $n$ weights at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{t}\text{: a set of assigned points at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\text{: a binary $n\times\{0,1\}$ matrix which encodes the greedy assignment at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{t}\text{: an $n\times t$ binary matrix.}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{t}\text{: an $n\times t$ binary matrix.}

We let
\boldsymbol{z}^{0}
be the all zero’s matrix,
\mathbf{A}^{0}
and
\mathbf{K}^{0}
be the empty
n\times 0
matrices, and
\mathbf{S}^{0}=\emptyset
. For time
1\leq t\leq t_{e}
, we instantiate
\mathbf{A}^{t}
and
\mathbf{K}^{t}
(which adds the
t
-th column to
\mathbf{A}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{K}^{t-1}
):

1.

For each
i\in[n]
, we set
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
to
\min\{w_{i},1/t\}
if
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, and
w_{i}
otherwise. Furthermore, we let
\gamma^{t}
be
1
if
t\leq t_{0}
, and
\min(\gamma/t,1)
otherwise.

2.

For each
i\in[n]
, if
t\leq t_{0}
let
\boldsymbol{g}^{t}_{i}=z^{*}_{i}
,
else for
t>t_{0}
, we let

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\boldsymbol{z}_{j,0}^{t-1}

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\boldsymbol{z}_{j,1}^{t-1}

and let
\boldsymbol{g}^{t}_{i}
be set to
(1,0)
if
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}<\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}
or
(0,1)
otherwise.

3.

Update the matrices
\mathbf{A}^{t}
and
\mathbf{K}^{t}
by setting

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{t}_{i,\ell}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}^{t-1}&\text{ if $\ell\leq t-1$}\\
\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})&\ell=t\\
\end{cases},\qquad\mathbf{K}^{t}_{i,\ell}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{K}_{i,\ell}^{t-1}&\text{ if $\ell\leq t-1$}\\
\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{\ell})&\ell=t\end{cases},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{t}=\mathbf{S}^{t-1}\cup\{i\text{ for which }\mathbf{A}^{t}_{i,t}=1\}

4.

For each
i\in[n]
, define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{t}_{i}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{z}^{t-1}_{i}&i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}\\
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}&i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}\land i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}\\
(0,0)&\text{else}\\
\end{cases}

5.

Let
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
, and consider a natural ordering
\pi
where
\pi_{j}
is the
j
-th element of
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
. Let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*}=\boldsymbol{z}^{*}_{\pi_{i}}

When
t>t_{e}
, the process completes and we output
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
, and we will refer to
\mathbf{A}^{t_{e}}
and
\mathbf{K}^{t_{e}}
as
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{K}
, respectively.

5.1
From
GreedyProcess
to
Assign

We collect the following observations, which allows us to connect the analysis of
GreedyProcess
to the assignment rule from Theorem
4
. In particular, we will show that the distribution over matrices
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K})
at the end of the above greedy process is exactly that which is defined for the statement of Theorem
4
. This allows us to prove Theorem
4
by showing that the partial cut produced by the greedy process is approximately optimal.

Observation 11
.

Since
\gamma^{\ell}=1
for all
\ell\leq t_{0}
,
\mathbf{K}_{i,\ell}^{t}=1
for all
\ell\leq t_{0}
and
i\in[n]
. Therefore,

\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}=\left\{j\in[n]:\exists\ell\leq t_{0}\text{ s.t }\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}=1\right\}=\left\{j\in[n]:\exists\ell\leq t_{0}\text{ s.t }\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1\right\}.

Therefore, by having access to pairs
(j,\ell)
and point
x_{j}
with
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1
and knowledge of
t_{0}
, one can construct the set
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
, identify the parameter
\boldsymbol{m}
, and determine the ordering
\pi
so that
\pi_{j}
denotes the
j
-th element of
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
.

Observation 12
.

Since the row
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}=z_{i}^{*}
for every
t\leq t_{0}
, letting
\pi
denote the natural ordering where
\pi_{j}
is the
j
-th element of
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
, for every
j\in[\boldsymbol{m}]
,

\boldsymbol{z}_{\pi(j)}=z_{\pi(j)}^{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(1,0)&\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}_{j}=0\\
(0,1)&\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}_{j}=1\end{array}\right..

Observation 13
.

For every
i\in[n]
, let
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
denote the smallest number where
\mathbf{A}_{i,\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}=1
. Then,
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
, and
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
depends on the values
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
, which may be computed from:

•

The point
x_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
,

•

The pairs
(j,\ell)
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}=1
and
\ell\leq\boldsymbol{t}_{i}-1
.

•

For every pair
(j,\ell)
as above, the point
x_{j}
, the weight
w_{j}
, and the smallest
\boldsymbol{t}_{j}
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\boldsymbol{t}_{j}}=1
, and
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}
(which may, itself, be recursively computed or from
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
).

In fact, the assignment rule is exactly that which is set by
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
in Figure
3
.

5.2
Proof of Theorem
4
assuming Lemma
5.1

Our main structural claim is showing that
GreedyProcess
produces an approximately optimal cut. We state the main lemma below and defer its proof. We show next that there is a local algorithm, such that, by receiving only limited information, can simulate the decisions of
GreedyProcess
and produce an approximately optimal cut.

Lemma 5.1

(Main Lemma)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
be the output of
GreedyProcess
((X,d),w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e},z^{*})
, where the weights
w
are
\lambda
-compatible. For any
\varepsilon>0
, if

\gamma\geq\dfrac{(\ln(t_{e})+1)^{2}\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad t_{0}\geq\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\cdot\lambda}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}.

Then,

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})]\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

We defer the proof of Lemma
5.1
to Section
6
, but we are now ready to conclude Theorem
4
, which combines the above observations, as well as the fact that, by setting
t_{e}\geq n\lambda/\varepsilon
, we may place any point
x_{i}
in the
0
-side of the cut (by letting
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=(1,0)
) as a default whenever
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is always 0 without degrading the quality of the cut.

Proof:
[Proof of Theorem
4
assuming Lemma
5.1
]
We first notice that, by construction of the greedy process, we have defined each row
\mathbf{A}_{i}
to behave independently and distributed from
\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
. In particular, note that
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is always sampled from
\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
, where the setting of
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
depends solely on whether
i
is or is not in
\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
; if it is not in
\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, it is set to
\min\{w_{i},1/t\}
and otherwise
w_{i}
. Note that
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
if and only if there exists
\ell\leq t-1
with
\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}=1
, coinciding with
\mathcal{T}(w_{i})
. The case of
\mathbf{K}_{i,t}
is simpler, as it is sampled from
\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
which is
1
if
t\leq t_{0}
and
\min\{\gamma/t,1\}
otherwise; similarly, from
\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
.

If we let
\boldsymbol{z}
denote the cut which was output by greedy process, we partition the coordinates into

\mathbf{G}=\left\{i\in[n]:\exists t\leq t_{e}\text{ s.t }\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{B}=[n]\setminus\mathbf{G}.

Importantly, we have defined
GreedyProcess
and
Assign
so that, whenever
i\in\mathbf{G}
, if we let
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
denote the activation time of point
x_{i}
, we have
\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})_{i}
, which is the output of
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
is the same as
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[\min_{\sigma}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))\right]\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right)\cdot\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[i\in\mathbf{B}\right].

(13)

We finally may apply Lemma
5.1
as well as

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[i\in\mathbf{B}\right]=\prod_{t=1}^{t_{e}}\left(1-\min\left\{w_{i},1/t\right\}\right)\leq\prod_{t=\lceil 1/w_{i}\rceil}^{t_{e}}\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\right)=\prod_{t=\lceil 1/w_{i}\rceil}^{t_{e}}\frac{t-1}{t}=\dfrac{\lceil 1/w_{i}\rceil-1}{t_{e}}.

Since
w_{i}\leq 1/2
, we have
\lceil 1/w_{i}\rceil-1\leq 1/w_{i}
, so the left-most term in (
13
) is at most

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{w_{i}}\leq\frac{\lambda\cdot n}{t_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{k}).

6
Proof of Lemma
5.1

As with the analysis of
[
MS08
]
, the analysis of the greedy process relies on a “fictitious cut” vector. The fictitious cut vector begins by encoding an optimal cut, and transforms into the cut that is output by the greedy process once it terminates. Even though the structure of the argument (in particular, tracking a fictitious cut) remains the same as
[
MS08
]
, the specification of the fictitious cut itself will differ, in order to correspond to our greedy process.

Notation.

For the remainder of this section, we will consider a fixed metric space
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
consisting of
n
points, as well as settings of the weight vector
w\in(0,1/2]^{n}
, parameters
\gamma\geq 1
,
t_{0},t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
and an optimal cut
z^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
. In the definitions below, we will reference random variables (such as
\boldsymbol{z}^{t},\boldsymbol{w}^{t},\mathbf{S}^{t}
, …, etc.) referring to an execution of
\textsc{GreedyProcess}((X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d),w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e},z^{*})
and a draw
(\boldsymbol{z},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K},\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})\sim\mathcal{D}^{*}(w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e})
.

Definition 6.1

(Fictitious cut)
.

The fictitious cut is specified by a sequence of random variables
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}\in[0,1]^{n\times\{0,1\}}
for each
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{e}\}
. We let:

•

For
t=0
, we define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0}=z^{*}
.

•

For
t>0
, we denote for each
i\in[n]
, the vector
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\{0,1\}}
by
7
7

7

Even though it is not yet clear, we will show that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\in[0,1]^{\{0,1\}}
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t}&i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}\\
\dfrac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\left(\dfrac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}\right)&\text{o.w.}\end{array}\right..

Claim 6.2
.

The following are simple facts about the fictitious cut, which follow from Definition
6.1
:

•

For every
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{0}\}
, we have
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}=z^{*}
.

•

For every
i\in[n]
, we have
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}}_{i,1}\geq\boldsymbol{z}_{i,1}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}}_{i,0}\geq\boldsymbol{z}_{i,0}
.

Proof:

The first item follows from the definition of
GreedyProcess
, as well as a simple inductive argument. For
t=0
, set
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0}=z^{*}
by Definition
6.1
, so assume such is the case for
t\in\{0,\dots,\tilde{t}\}
for
\tilde{t}<t_{0}
, and we will establish it for
t=\tilde{t}+1
. Recall that, for every
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{0}\}
, Line
2
of
GreedyProcess
sets
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}=z^{*}_{i}
. So consider the two cases which may happen in (
•
‣
6.1
); if
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}
, then
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t}
, which is equal to
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=z^{*}_{i}
for
i\in\mathbf{S}_{t}\subset\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
(by Observation
12
). If
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}
, then (
•
‣
6.1
) gives

\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=\frac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot z_{i}^{*}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot z_{i}^{*}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\cdot z_{i}^{*}\right)=z_{i}^{*}.

For the second item, notice that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t_{e}}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
if
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}
, and otherwise lies in
[0,1]^{\{0,1\}}
, whereas
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=(0,0)
.

Recall that, for the proof of Lemma
5.1
, we seek to upper bound the expected difference between
f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})
. Thus, the definition of the fictitious cut, as well as Claim
6.2
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0})\right]=\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})\right],

(16)

where in the first inequality, the fact
f(\boldsymbol{z})\leq f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}})
follows from definition of
f
, the fact distances are non-negative, and Claim
6.2
. We will focus on showing an upper bound for each
t\in\{t_{0}+1,\dots,t_{e}\}
, and by symmetry of
d(\cdot,\cdot)
and definition of
f
, we may write

\displaystyle f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t-1}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t-1}

(17)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t-1}\right)

(18)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t-1}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t-1}\right),

(19)

Lemma 6.3
.

For every
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{e}\}
and any fixed execution of the first
t-1
steps of
GreedyProcess
, then for every
i\notin S^{t-1}
and any
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right]=\frac{1}{t}\cdot\left(g_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right),

where the expectation above is only over the randomness which defines
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}
after fixing the first
t-1
steps of the process (thereby fixing
\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}
and
g_{i,b}^{t}
). Furthermore, the expectation of expressions (
18
) and (
19
) is at most

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2t^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

Proof:

Consider any fixed execution of the first
t-1
steps of
GreedyProcess
which fixes the setting of
\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}
, the setting of
w_{i}^{t}
(in Line
1
), and
g_{i,b}^{t}
(in Line
2
), as these depend only on the randomness which occurred up to step
t-1
. We now consider what happens to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}
at step
t
whenever
i\notin S^{t-1}
:

•

With probability
w_{i}^{t}
, we have
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}
, so (
•
‣
6.1
) sets
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=z_{i}^{t-1}
, which is equal to
g_{i}^{t}
according to Line
4
:

\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}=g_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}.

•

With probability
1-w_{i}^{t}
, we have
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}
, and

\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}=\frac{1}{1-w_{i}^{t}}\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot g_{i,b}^{t}-w_{i}^{t}g_{i,b}^{t}\right)-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}.

Thus, taking expectation with respect to the randomness in this step
t
(namely, the samples in Line
3
), after some cancellations:

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\cdot\left(g_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right).

This gives the first part of the lemma. For the second part, we note that for any execution of the first
t-1
steps, any
i\in S^{t-1}
, will have
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}
in Line
3
, and therefore
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i}=\widehat{z}_{i}^{t-1}=z_{i}^{t-1}
. In other words, may re-write the expression (
18
) and (
19
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\notin S^{t-1}}\sum_{j\notin S^{t-1}}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right).

Since
d(x_{i},x_{j})=0
if
i=j
, we have, by independence when
i\neq j
, we have that the expectation over the step
t
is

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{g_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}}{t}\cdot\frac{g_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}}{t}\leq\frac{1}{t^{2}},

since
g_{\cdot,b}^{t},\widehat{z}_{\cdot,b}^{t-1}\in[0,1]
.

Lemma
6.3
will handle the upper bound for (
18
) and (
19
), and we now work on upper bounding (
17
). We will now follow the approach of
[
MS08
]
and consider a contribution matrix and an estimated contribution; this will help us relate (
17
) to how the
GreedyProcess
evolves.

Definition 6.4

(Contribution Matrix and Estimated Contribution)
.

For time
t\in\{1,\dots,t_{e}\}
, the contribution matrix
\boldsymbol{c}^{t}\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
is given by letting,

\boldsymbol{c}^{t}_{i,0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j,0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{c}^{t}_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j,1}.

For a time
t\in\{1,\dots,t_{e}\}
and an index
j\in[n]
, we define
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{0}=1
and for
t\geq 2

\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}=\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}},

and the estimated contribution matrix
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
be given by

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}_{i,0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t-1},

and notice that, since
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t-1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{j,0}^{t-1}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t-1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{j,1}^{t-1}
when
j\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
(and equivalently, when
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}
is non-zero), we defined
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}
to align with Line
2
in
GreedyProcess
.

The purpose of defining the contribution matrix and the expected contribution matrix is in order to upper bound (
17
). In fact, we continuing on the upper bound, we may write (focusing on the
0
-side of the cut),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}\right)+\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,0}\right)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t},

where we used the fact that, in
GreedyProcess
and (
•
‣
6.1
),
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
will mean
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t-1}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t-1}
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t}
, so
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}
. By the first part of Lemma
6.3
, we can simplify

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}\right)\right]+\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\frac{1}{t}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}|\right]+\frac{1}{t}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}\right]

(20)

where have used the first part of Lemma
6.3
and took expectation over the randomness of step
t
, and the fact that
\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t},\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\in[0,1]
to place the absolute values. We conclude the proof of Lemma
5.1
by stating the following two lemmas (whose proof we defer shortly).

Lemma 6.5
.

For any execution of
GreedyProcess
, any
t\in\{t_{0}+1,\dots,t_{e}\}
and any
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
,

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,0}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}+\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,1}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}\leq 0.

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
t\in\{2,\dots,t_{e}\}
and any
b\in\{0,1\}
, whenever
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}\right|\right]\leq 10\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\gamma}}{t-1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}.

Proof:
[Proof of Lemma
5.1
assuming Lemma
6.5
and
6.6
]
We put all the elements together, where we seek to upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})\right]\leq\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\right)\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t-1}\right)\boldsymbol{c}_{i,1}^{t}\right]+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right)\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\frac{1}{t^{2}},

where we first followed (
16
), and then simplified (
18
) and (
19
) using the second part of Lemma
6.3
. Then, using (
20
) and Lemma
6.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\left(\frac{1}{t}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,0}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}|\right]+\frac{1}{t}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,1}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}|\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+O(1/t_{0})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\ln(t_{e})+1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}+\dfrac{\sqrt{\gamma}}{t_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{t_{0}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right).

We note that the assumptions on our weight vector imply that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\lambda}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

so the above error bound simplifies to

\left(\frac{(\ln(t_{e})+1)\sqrt{\lambda}}{\sqrt{\gamma}}+\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\cdot\lambda}}{t_{0}}+\frac{1}{t_{0}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

that when

\gamma\geq\frac{(\ln(t_{e})+1)^{2}\cdot\lambda}{\varepsilon^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad t_{0}\geq\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\cdot\lambda}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\},

we obtain our desired bound.

6.1
Proof of Lemma
6.5

The proof of Lemma
6.5
proceeds by first showing that the fictitious cut
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}\in[0,1]^{n\times\{0,1\}}
. The proof is a straight-forward induction. This allows us to say that the greedy choices
\boldsymbol{g}^{t}
are chosen so as to make the expression in Lemma
6.5
negative.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
t\in\{0,\dots t_{e}\}
and any
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}=1

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}\right|\leq 1,

which implies
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}\in[0,1]^{n\times\{0,1\}}
.

Proof:

The proof proceeds by induction on
t
. We note that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0}=z^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
, so the condition trivially holds. We assume for induction that the condition holds for all
t\leq\tilde{t}
and show the condition for
t=\tilde{t}+1
. First, note that if
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}
, then
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t}\in\{0,1\}^{\{0,1\}}
and satisfies the condition. So, suppose
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\left(\dfrac{t-1}{t}\cdot\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}+\boldsymbol{z}_{i,1}^{t-1}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{t}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\right)\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}+\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\left(\frac{t-1}{t}+\frac{1}{t}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\right)=1,

where we used the inductive hypothesis and the fact
\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}+\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}=1
. For the difference, we have:

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,0}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,1}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\dfrac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\left(\dfrac{t-1}{t}\cdot\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{i,1}^{t-1}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{t}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\right)\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}-\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\right|\cdot\left(\frac{t-1}{t}+\left|\frac{1}{t}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\right|\right).

Finally, we note that
GreedyProcess
enforces that
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\leq 1/t
when
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, so
(t-1)/t+|1/t-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}|=1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
. Giving the upper bound on the difference.

To conclude the proof of Lemma
6.5
, recall that
\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}
are chosen so as to minimize

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}+\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]\\
\alpha+\beta=1\end{subarray}}\alpha\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}+\beta\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}\leq\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t-1}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t-1}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t},

which gives the desired bound.

6.2
Proof of Lemma
6.6

It will be useful for the proof of Lemma
6.6
to re-index the time so that we upper bound, for all
t\in\{1,\dots,t_{e}-1\}
, the quantity

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right|\right],

because the definition of
\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}
depends solely on the randomness up to step
t
, i.e., those from
\mathbf{A}^{t}_{j,\ell},\mathbf{K}^{t}_{j,\ell}
for all
j\neq i
and
\ell\leq t
. We interpret
\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}
as the contribution of point
i
to the
b
-th side of the cut once all points activated by time
t
have been assigned according to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}
, and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}
is meant to be an estimate of that contribution. It is natural to define the error in the estimation coming from point
j
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t}\right)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{t}}{\boldsymbol{w}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}

\displaystyle\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)=\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}.

Note that the case
t=0
,
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{1}(j)=0
by definition of
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{0}=1
for all
j
.
By Jensen’s inequality and the definition of
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}|\right]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1})^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2}.

(21)

Lemma 6.8
.

Consider any
t\geq 1
and any execution of the first
t-1
steps of
GreedyProcess
, fixing the random variables
A^{t-1},K^{t-1},\widehat{z}^{t-1}
, …, etc. Then, considering only the
t
-th step of
GreedyProcess
,

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)\right]=\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{t}(j),

where the expectation is taken only with respected to the randomness in the
t
-th step. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right]=0.

Proof:

We simply go through the calculation, where we write
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)
in terms of
R_{j}^{t-1}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)=d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\cdot\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}}{w_{j}^{t}\cdot\gamma^{t}}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot R_{j}^{t-1}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j,b}.

Note that,
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}
, for all
j
is the only source of randomness in the
t
-th time step; in particular,
R_{j}^{t-1}
is fixed, and
w_{j}^{t}
is fixed (and hence unbolded); however,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j}
does depend on
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}
if
j\notin S^{t-1}
. We now consider two cases, depending on whether or not
j\in S^{t-1}
. In the case that
j\in S^{t-1}
, the fictitious cut
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j,b}
will always be set to
\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{j,b}
(as it remains fixed), and draw
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(w_{i}^{t})
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
. Therefore, the expectation over the draw of
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}
results in

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\cdot\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}}{w_{j}^{t}\cdot\gamma^{t}}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot R_{j}^{t-1}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j,b}\right]

\displaystyle=d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\frac{1}{t}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot R_{j}^{t-1}\right)\widehat{z}^{t-1}_{j,b}

\displaystyle=\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-R_{j}^{t-1}\right)\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{t}(j).

On the other hand, if
j\notin S^{t-1}
, then
R_{j}^{t-1}=0
. The expectation calculation, however, is more complicated than two Bernoulli’s (as above), since the setting of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j,b}
depends on the realization of
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}
. We go through the two cases:

•

With probability
w_{j}^{t}
, we have
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}=1
. This results in a setting of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j,b}=\boldsymbol{z}^{t}_{j,b}
according to the first case of (
•
‣
6.1
), and
\boldsymbol{z}^{t}_{j,b}=g_{j,b}^{t}
from Line
4
. Taking expectation over
\mathbf{K}_{j,t}^{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
, we have that
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)
becomes

\displaystyle d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\frac{1}{t}\cdot\frac{1}{w_{j}^{t}}\right)\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}.

•

With probability
1-w_{j}^{t}
, we have
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}=0
, in which case
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}
is updated according to the second case of (
•
‣
6.1
), and
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)
becomes:

\displaystyle d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\frac{1}{1-w_{j}^{t}}\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}-w_{j}^{t}\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}\right).

All together, this gives us an expectation of
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)
is

\displaystyle d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(w_{j}^{t}\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}-\frac{1}{t}\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}+\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}-w_{j}^{t}\cdot g_{j,b}^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{t}(j),

since
R_{j}^{t-1}=0
. Taking the sum over terms concludes the lemma.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any
t\in\{2,\dots,t_{e}-1\}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right|\right]\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\ell^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2}.

Proof:

We will upper bound (
21
), where we first prove by induction on
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{e}-1\}
that we always satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right)^{2}\right]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\frac{\ell^{2}}{t^{2}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}\right].

Note that the base case of
t=0
corresponds to
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{1}
, which is deterministically
0
by
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{0}=1
. Thus, assume for the sake of induction that

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right)^{2}\right]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\frac{\ell^{2}}{(t-1)^{2}}\cdot\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}\right].

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}+\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right)^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\right)^{2}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t})^{2}\right],

where we expand and use Lemma
6.8
to say

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right)\right]=0,

since in particular, it holds for every setting of
\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{t}
. Applying the inductive hypothesis:

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t}\right)^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{t-1}{t}\right)^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\frac{\ell^{2}}{(t-1)^{2}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\frac{\ell^{2}}{t^{2}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}\right].

We now expand
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)
, and the fact that
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)
and
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j^{\prime})
are independent to conclude, by Lemma
6.8
once more, the desired inequality.

Lemma 6.10
.

Fix any
\ell>1
and any
i,j\in[n]
with
w_{j}\leq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 61\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\dfrac{1}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}.

Proof:

We will compute the expectation by first considering any fixed execution of the first
\ell-1
steps, and then take the expectation over the randomness in the
\ell
-th step. Note that, by fixing the first
\ell-1
steps, we write:

\displaystyle\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)

\displaystyle=d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}}{w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot R_{j}^{\ell-1}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{\ell},

\displaystyle\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)

\displaystyle=d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\left(1-R_{j}^{\ell-1}\right)\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1}.

In the case that
j\in S^{\ell-1}
, we have fixed the setting of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{\ell}=\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1}
, by Line
1
,
w_{j}^{\ell}=w_{j}
. The expectation, solely over the randomness in the draw of
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(w_{j})
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{\ell})
, is

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[{\bf 1}\{j\in S^{\ell-1}\}\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad={\bf 1}\{j\in S^{\ell-1}\}\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\frac{(\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1})^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}\left(1-\frac{1}{w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)^{2}\leq\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}.

(22)

So, we now consider the case that
j\notin S^{\ell-1}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(w_{j}^{\ell})
for
w_{j}^{\ell}=\min\{w_{j},1/\ell\}
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{\ell})
. Similarly to the proof of Lemma
6.8
, the expectation is slightly more complicated, as
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{\ell}
depends on the realization of
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
. However, the fact
j\notin S^{\ell-1}
means
R_{j}^{\ell-1}=0
, which simplifies the expression as well. Consider the three cases:

•

With probability
w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}
, both
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
are both set to
1
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{\ell}
is set to
g_{j,b}^{\ell}
. So,

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell\cdot w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)g_{j,b}^{\ell}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{\ell\cdot w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)^{2},

where we used the fact
w_{j}^{\ell}\leq 1/\ell
, as well as the fact
g_{j,b}^{\ell},\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1}\in[0,1]
.

•

With probability
w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot(1-\gamma^{\ell})
,
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
is set to
1
, but
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
is set to
0
. In this case,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{\ell}
is set to
g_{j,b}^{\ell}
, and we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\left(g_{j,b}^{\ell}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1}\right)^{2}\leq 4\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}

•

With probability
1-w_{j}^{\ell}
, we set
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
to
0
(irrespective of the draw of
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
), in which case
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{\ell}
is updated according to the second case in Definition
6.1
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{w_{j}^{\ell}}{1-w_{j}^{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\widehat{z}^{\ell-1}_{j,b}+\frac{1}{\ell}\cdot g_{j,b}^{\ell}-w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot g_{j,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\left(\dfrac{4}{\ell}\right)^{2},

where we use the fact
w_{j}^{\ell}\leq\min\{1/\ell,1/2\}
to obtain our desired bound.

Once we combine the above three cases (while multiplying by their respective probabilities), the first case, when
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}=1
, dominates the calculation. In particular, when we compute the expectation solely over the randomness in
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{K}_{j,\ell}^{\ell}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[{\bf 1}\{j\notin S^{\ell-1}\}\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{\bf 1}\{j\notin S^{\ell-1}\}\cdot\dfrac{30\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}.

The above bounds the expectations over the randomness solely over step
\ell
. However, for large
\ell
,
w_{j}^{\ell}=1/\ell
which weakens the bound (contrast this with (
22
) where
w_{j}^{\ell}
is replaced by
w_{j}
). However, we exploit the fact
j\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}
with probability which decays for large
\ell
. In particular, whenever
\ell-1\geq 1/w_{j}
, we may obtain the following bound on the probability
j\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[j\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}\right]=\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell-1}\left(1-\min\{w_{j},1/\ell^{\prime}\}\right)\leq\left(1-w_{j}\right)^{\lfloor 1/w_{j}\rfloor}\dfrac{\lceil 1/w_{j}\rceil-1}{\ell-1}\leq\frac{1}{w_{j}\cdot(\ell-1)}.

(23)

Applying this to the expectation over the time steps up to
\ell-1
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}+\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[j\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}\right]\cdot\dfrac{30\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot\min\{w_{j},1/\ell\}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}},

and this value is at most

\frac{31\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}

whenever
w_{j}^{\ell}=\min\{1/\ell,w_{j}\}=w_{j}
. Otherwise, we have
w_{j}^{\ell}=1/\ell
and this occurs when
\ell>1
since
w_{j}\leq 1/2
. The above expectation becomes at most

\displaystyle\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}+\dfrac{1}{(\ell-1)\cdot w_{j}}\cdot\frac{30\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\leq\frac{61\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}.

Combining Lemma
6.9
and
6.10
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(61\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{10}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\gamma^{\ell}}\right)^{1/2}

(24)

and we have,

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\gamma^{\ell}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\left(1+\gamma+\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\ell\right)^{1/2}\leq 2\sqrt{\gamma}+\frac{t}{\sqrt{\gamma}},

which means that plugging in to the above bound gives us our desired bound of

\displaystyle 10\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\gamma}}{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}.

7
Dynamic Streaming Algorithm

In dynamic geometric streaming algorithms, a low-space streaming algorithm will receive an input set of points
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
presented as a stream. The stream may consist of both insertions and deletions of individual points
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, and the algorithm maintains a memory state for points currently in the set. We associate (multi)-sets
X
from
[\Delta]^{d}
by vectors
\mathrm{vec}(X)
of integer coordinates with
\Delta^{d}
coordinates. Each coordinate corresponds to a point in
[\Delta]^{d}
, and counts the number of times that point appears in the multi-set. An update specifies a coordinate
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
and a number
\delta\in\{-1,1\}
, and the underlying vector is updated by increasing the
x
-th coordinate by
\delta
(i.e., inserting or deleting a point). With this encoding, all of our algorithm will be
linear sketches
, they are given by
\mathbf{S}\cdot\mathrm{vec}(X)
for a random matrix
\mathbf{S}
. However, we will use the fact that the final vector
\mathrm{vec}(X)\in\{0,1\}^{[\Delta]^{d}}
corresponds to a set (and there are no duplicate points).

Geometric Sketches and Geometric Sampling Sketches.

We state the two preliminary components. The first will be the geometric sampling sketches from
[
CJK23
]
, and the second an algorithm to estimate the geometric weights. These sketches play the role of (i) sampling the “active and kept” points, i.e., those
x_{i}\in X
with
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{i,t}=1
(for some
t
) in Theorem
4
, and (ii) computing the metric-compatible weights as in Section
3
and Section
4
.

Lemma 7.1

(Geometric Sampling Sketches (Lemma 4.2
[
CJK23
]
))
.

There is a randomized streaming algorithm initialized with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\in(0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
p\geq 1
,
\Delta,d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
s\in\mathbbm{N}
uses
\mathrm{poly}(s\cdot d\log(\Delta/\delta)/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})
space and satisfies the following:

•

It receives an input dataset
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
of at least two distinct points as a dynamic stream.

•

Then, it outputs
s
pairs
(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j})
of random variables
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j}\in X\cup\{\bot\}
, together with a number
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j}\in[0,1]
.

With probability at most
\delta
, the algorithm fails (in a manner which is undetectable). If it doesn’t fail, then each sample
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j}
is independent. With probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\delta^{-1}s\Delta^{d})
, for every
x\in X
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}_{(\boldsymbol{y}_{j}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{j}^{*})}\left[\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j}=x\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}p_{j}(x)\geq\frac{1}{\mathsf{D}}\cdot\dfrac{\sum_{y\in X}d_{\ell_{p}}(x,y)}{\sum_{y\in X}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in X}d_{\ell_{p}}(y,y^{\prime})},

for known
\mathsf{D}=\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\delta)
. Whenever
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j}\neq\bot
, then
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j}
satisfies

\displaystyle p_{j}(\boldsymbol{y}_{j}^{*})\leq\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j}\leq(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})p_{j}(\boldsymbol{y}_{j}^{*}).

Lemma
7.1
gives an algorithm to sample from
X
with probability according to distances within
X
. We will refer to the distribution of the
j
-th sample generated from a call to Lemma
7.1
conditioned on a successful initialization as
\mathcal{G}_{j}(X)
. In the analysis, we set the failure probability
\delta
to
\varepsilon
, and we will condition on a successful initialization of Lemma
7.1
. The case of an unsuccessful initialization is considered a “catastrophic” failure (but are rare enough so as to not affect the algorithm significantly).

Remark 14

(Additive Error in Lemma 4.2 of
[
CJK23
]
)
.

The statement of Lemma
7.1
differs slightly from Lemma 4.2 in
[
CJK23
]
, but the changes are solely cosmetic. We allow
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\in(0,1)
and always overestimate, bound the failure probability by
\delta
(instead of
.01
in Lemma 4.2 of
[
CJK23
]
) by paying a
1/\delta
factor in
\mathsf{D}
, and replace
\lambda
with
\mathsf{D}
. Similarly, we bound the probability of an individual sample failing by
1-\mathrm{poly}(\delta/(s\Delta^{d}))
(instead of
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\Delta^{d})
in Lemma 4.2 of
[
CJK23
]
) by paying an additional
\mathrm{poly}(\log(s\delta^{-1}))
bits of space. By setting
\delta\leq\varepsilon
, the probability that any of the samples fail is at most
\varepsilon
, which would contribute at most
\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})
total distance to the expected cut value. We will henceforth assume that the sampling procedure succeeds.

Lemma 7.2

(Weight Sketches)
.

There is a randomized streaming algorithm initialized with
p\geq 1
,
\Delta,d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, which uses
\mathrm{poly}(d\log(\Delta/\delta))
words of space, and for a known parameter
\mathsf{D}=\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta)
, satisfies the following:

•

It receives an input set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
of at least two distinct points as a dynamic stream.

•

Given a point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, it outputs a number
\boldsymbol{w}(x)
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
over the randomness in construction, every
x\in X
satisfies

\dfrac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x)}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}\leq\boldsymbol{w}(x)\leq\mathsf{D}\cdot\dfrac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x)}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}.

Proof:

We describe a sketch which will process the dataset
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
and will be able to produce, for every
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, a
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta)
-approximation to the quantity
\boldsymbol{\eta}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x)
, which is the desired numerator of the quantity
\boldsymbol{w}(x)
above. The denominator, which is the sum of all distances, can also be estimated up to a factor of
2
within the space bound (in particular, it the value of the max-cut output by
[
CJK23
]
would be one such estimate). In order to approximate
\boldsymbol{\eta}(x)
, we combine two ingredients:

•

The first is Fact 1 from
[
Ind04
]
(and Lemma 4.3 in
[
CJK23
]
) which shows an embedding into a probabilistic tree
\mathbf{T}
(equivalently, an embedding into
\ell_{1}
) such that
d_{\mathbf{T}}(x,y)\geq d_{\ell_{p}}(x,y)
and
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{T}}\left[d_{\mathbf{T}}(x,y)\right]\leq O(d\log\Delta)d_{\ell_{p}}(x,y)
for any
x,y\in[\Delta]^{d}
.

•

The second is the Median Cost Evaluation sketch (MediEval) from
[
Ind04
]
, which can process a dataset of points
X
such that for a set
C
of at most
k
points, the
k
-median cost of clustering
X
with the center set
C
can be recovered.

In
[
Ind04
]
, the MediEval sketch is described for two-dimensional point sets, but it follows immediately for evaluating
k
-median distances in a tree metric as well. Our sketch will consider
L=O(d\log\Delta)
draws of probabilistic tree embeddings
\mathbf{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{T}_{L}
as well as MediEval sketches for the dataset points in each tree instantiated to succeed with probability at least
1-\delta/(\Delta^{d}\cdot L)
, instantiated for
k=1
. Note that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x)
is the
k
-median with center
x
, so each of the
L
trees gives us an estimate of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\mathbf{T}_{\ell}}(x_{i},x)
, which is at least
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x)
and has expectation at most
O(d\log\Delta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x)
. Hence, using the minimum over all
\ell\in[L]
of the estimates
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\mathbf{T}_{\ell}}(x_{i},x)
gives the desired approximation to
\boldsymbol{\eta}(x)
.

We will use Lemma
7.2
in order to generate the metric-compatible weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
which we use in order to activate points. The main difference between the lemmas used in prior sections (namely, Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
4.1
) and Lemma
7.2
is that the above lemma works in the dynamic geometric streaming model and obtains a multiplicative
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta)
-approximation, as opposed to
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximations for
p\in[1,2]
. We also state the following lemma from
[
CJK23
]
, which will be used to generate samples from
X
distributed according to metric-compatible weights.

7.1
Activation Timelines and Masks for Dynamic Streams

In this section, we state the main structural theorem that is needed for controlling the cut quality of the dynamic streaming algorithm (analogous to Theorem
4
). The structural theorem will refer to weights generated according to Lemma
7.2
and the distribution generated from Lemma
7.1
, so that it is tailored to what can be achieved algorithmically for dynamic streams. We first define an appropriate version of activation timelines and masks, adapting Definition
2.2
and Definition
2.3
, as well as a timeline-mask summary, adapting Definition
2.5
. Then, we define a new assignment procedure, and state the main theorem.

Definition 7.3

(Correlated Timeline-Mask)
.

Let
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
,
t_{0},t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\gamma>1
and
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\in(0,1)
. A correlated timeline-mask is sampled according to the following procedure:

1.

Sample a single mask
\mathbf{K}\sim\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
from Definition
2.3
, let
s
denote the number of entries of
\mathbf{K}
set to
1
.

2.

Initialize an algorithm from Lemma
7.2
with
\delta=\varepsilon
, and let
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}=\boldsymbol{w}(x_{i})/(2\mathsf{D})
for all
i\in[n]
. Similarly, initialize an algorithm from Lemma
7.1
with
s
(coming from
\mathbf{K}
above) and
\delta=\varepsilon
, and let
(\boldsymbol{y}_{j}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j})
for
j\in[s]
.

3.

Iterate through
t=1,\dots,t_{e}
to generate
\mathbf{A}_{1,t},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n,t}
:

(a)

If
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
, each
i\in[n]
proceeds independently by letting

\boldsymbol{w}^{t}_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\min\{\boldsymbol{w}_{i},1/t\}&\forall\ell<t,\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}=0\\
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}&\text{o.w.}\end{array}\right.,

and then setting
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
independently.

(b)

If
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, use a sample
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*})
from Line
2
(re-indexed, see Remark
15
), with error parameter
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}
and failure probability
\varepsilon
. If
i^{*}\in[n]
is such that
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t}=x_{i^{*}}
, let

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{i^{*}}^{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\min\{\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t},1/t\}&\forall\ell<t,\mathbf{A}_{i^{*},\ell}=0\\
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t}&\text{o.w.}\end{array}\right..

Let
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=0
if
i\neq i^{*}
(or all
i
in case
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t}=\bot
). If
\mathbf{A}_{i^{*},\ell}=0
for all
\ell<t
, sample
\boldsymbol{b}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\min\{1/(t\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}),1\})
and set
\mathbf{A}_{i^{*},t}=\boldsymbol{b}_{t}
.
8
8

8

It is not exactly the case
\mathbf{A}_{i^{*},t}
is one with probability
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{i^{*}}^{t}
because
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}
is not exactly the probability that
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i^{*}}
.
Otherwise, let
\mathbf{A}_{i^{*},t}=1
.

As in Definition
2.2
, the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
for
\mathbf{A}_{i}
is the smallest
t
with
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
.

Remark 15

(Re-indexing the Geometric Samples
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})
)
.

Given a mask
\mathbf{K}
and
s=\|\mathbf{K}\|_{1}
geometric samples
\{(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j})\}\sim\mathcal{G}_{s}(X)
(where
j\in[s]
) via Lemma
7.1
. Each sample corresponds to a non-zero entry of
\mathbf{K}
. In the algorithm and later the analysis, it will be convenient to reference these samples using the index of the corresponding non-zero-entry in the mask. Hence we can re-index the samples
(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j})\rightarrow(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})
where
j
-th sample gets indexed by time
t
where
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is the
j
-th non-zero entry of
\mathbf{K}
.

The remainder of the section mirrors that of Section
2
. The correlated timeline-mask from Definition
7.3
serves the purpose of the activation timelines and masks from Definition
2.2
and
2.3
. Furthermore, the
n
timelines
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
sampled from Definition
7.3
define a seed (see Definition
2.4
). We define an analogous version of Definition
2.5
, where the same mask
\mathbf{K}
is used for
\mathbf{K}_{j}
for all
j\in[n]
.

Definition 7.4

(Correlated Timeline-Mask Summary)
.

Consider
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
,
t_{e},t_{0}\in\mathbbm{N}
and
\gamma\geq 1
.

•

Sample a correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
from Definition
7.3
.

•

The summary of
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
is given by the set of tuples

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}

\displaystyle=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left\{(x_{j},\ell;p_{\ell},t_{j}):\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1\\
\text{$t_{j}$ is the activation time of $\mathbf{A}_{j}$}\\
p_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{\ell}\text{ for $(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{\ell})$ produced by Line~\ref{en:k-1} in Definition~\ref{def:corr-timeline-mask}}\end{array}\right\}.

We present the assignment rule for the dynamic streaming algorithm,
Assign
, in Figure
11
. The assignment rule is analogous to that of Figure
3
which we used for Theorem
4
. The only difference between Figure
3
and Figure
11
is in the use of a correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
from Definition
7.3
and the correlated timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
from Definition
7.4
.

Assignment Rule

Assign
{}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
. The correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
from Definition
7.3
has been sampled.
\mathbf{P}
is the correlated timeline-mask summary from Definition
7.4
and
\sigma
is a seed for
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
(see Definition
2.4
). Since the timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
is completely determined by
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
, we refer to the rule as
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
for convenience.

Input
: The point
x_{i}
to be assigned and the activation time
t_{i}
for the timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}
.

Inductive Hypothesis
: We assume the algorithm has access to a partial cut
\boldsymbol{z}=z(\sigma,\mathbf{P},t_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\times\{0,1\}}
which assigns every point
x_{j}
which participates in a tuple of
\mathbf{P}
and whose activation time
t_{j}
is smaller than
t_{i}
.

Output
: The assignment, either
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
corresponding to the 0-side or 1-side of the cut, for the
i
-th point
x_{i}
.

•

Base Case
: If
t_{i}\leq t_{0}
, the point
x_{i}
appears in
\mathbf{P}
(and so does all of
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
). Hence, the index
\pi(x_{i})\in[m]
can be determined. Output
(1,0)
if
\sigma_{\pi(x_{i})}=0
and
(0,1)
if
\sigma_{\pi(x_{i})}=1
.

•

Incorporating Estimates
: Otherwise,
t_{i}>t_{0}
and we proceed as follows:

1.

As per Definition
7.3
for every
(x_{j},\ell;p_{\ell},t_{j})\in\mathbf{P}
, we let
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}=\min\{p_{\ell},1/\ell\}
if
\ell=t_{j}
and
p_{\ell}
if
\ell>t_{j}
. As per Definition
2.3
, we let
\gamma^{\ell}=\min\{\gamma/\ell,1\}
. We consider the subset of pairs

\mathbf{P}_{t_{i}}=\left\{(x_{j},\ell):(x_{j},\ell;p_{\ell},t_{j})\in\mathbf{P}\text{ and }\ell\leq t_{j}<t_{i}\right\}

which have been assigned by the inductive hypothesis (by recursively executing
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{j},t_{j})
).

2.

We compute the estimated contributions, from the
0
-side and
1
-side of the cut, at the time
t_{i}
:

\mathbf{C}_{0}=\sum_{(x_{j},\ell)\in\mathbf{P}_{t_{i}}}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}_{j,0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{C}_{1}=\sum_{(x_{j},\ell)\in\mathbf{P}_{t_{i}}}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}_{j,1}.

Output
(0,1)
if
\mathbf{C}_{0}>\mathbf{C}_{1}
(to assign
x_{i}
to the
1
-side of the cut), and
(1,0)
otherwise (to assign
x_{i}
to the
0
-side of the cut).

Figure 11:
The Assignment Procedure
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
for Dynamic Streaming Algorithms

Theorem 16

(Main Structural for Dynamic Streams)
.

For any set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
and
p\geq 1
, let
\mathsf{D}=\mathrm{poly}(d\log(\Delta)/\varepsilon)
from Lemma
7.1
and
7.2
, then let

t_{e}\geq\frac{n\cdot\mathsf{D}}{\varepsilon}\qquad\gamma\geq\dfrac{\left(\mathsf{D}\ln(t_{e})+1\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\qquad t_{0}\geq\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma}\cdot\mathsf{D}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\ln(t_{e})}

Consider the following setup:

•

Draw a correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
from Definition
7.3
.

•

For any seed
\sigma
of
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
, let
\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma)
denote the following cut:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma)_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})&\boldsymbol{t}_{i}\text{ is the activation time for }\mathbf{A}_{i}\\
(1,0)&\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=0\text{ for all $t\in[t_{e}]$}\end{array}\right.,

where we use the algorithm
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
from Figure
11
.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[\min_{\sigma}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))\right]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}).

with
f
from (
2
) with
\ell_{p}
-metric.

7.2
Dynamic Streaming Algorithm

Given Theorem
16
, we will show how to design a dynamic streaming algorithm which can maintain a low-memory space while receiving an input set
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
as a dynamic stream; execute a preprocessing step which can build the correlated timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}
and provide query access to activation times, and select an approximately minimum seed
\sigma
; and finally, provide query access to the assignment specified by
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma}(x_{i},t_{i})
from Figure
11
.

Theorem 17

(Euclidean Max-Cut in Dynamic Streams)
.

For any
p\geq 1
,
d,\Delta\in\mathbbm{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a randomized streaming algorithm with the following guarantees:

•

Maintenance
: The streaming algorithm receives an input set
X\subset[\Delta]^{d}
as a dynamic stream, and uses a random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
and
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
words of space.

•

Query
: For a point
x\in[\Delta]^{d}
, the sub-routine
\textsc{Assign}(x)
outputs
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
corresponding to the
0
-side or the
1
-side of the cut.

If, after receiving the set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
, we let
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
be given by

\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{Assign}(x_{i}),

then

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\boldsymbol{f}(z)\right]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}),

using
f
from (
2
) with
\ell_{p}
-metric.

Maintenance and Updates.

The memory contents of the dynamic streaming algorithm,
DS-E-Max-Cut
, are simple to describe, and the non-trivial part of Theorem
17
is showing how to unpack the memory contents to apply Theorem
16
. In particular, the algorithm is initialized with parameters
\Delta,d\in\mathbbm{N}
,
p\geq 1
and an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. The algorithm will maintain:

•

A single instance of the streaming algorithm from Lemma
7.2
, initialized with
\delta=\varepsilon/\Delta^{d}
.

•

An instance of the streaming algorithm from Lemma
7.1
, initialized with parameters derived from the initialization,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\ln(t_{e})}
,
\delta=\varepsilon
, and
s=\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
which is set large enough so as to be more than the number of entries in
\mathbf{K}
will be
1
.

•

A pointer to a random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
.

Whenever a point is inserted or removed from the stream, the corresponding update is performed on the streaming algorithm from Lemma
7.2
, as well as the instance of Lemma
7.1
. The space complexity is trivially bounded by
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
, since
s=\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
instances of Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
7.1
, each of which uses
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
words.

Preprocessing: Generating the Summary and Accessing Activation Times.

We now give a procedure which will unpack the memory contents of
DS-E-Max-Cut
in order to “implicitly” sample a correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
(see Definition
7.3
). Here, “implicit” refers to the fact that the algorithm does not need to output the
(n+1)\cdot t_{e}
bits which specify
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
. We will show that the algorithm may produce the correlated timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K})
(see Definition
7.4
) and provide access to the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
for the timeline
\mathbf{A}_{i}
when given the point
x_{i}
. This is all that the algorithm will need in order to execute
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},t_{i})
.

We will utilize the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}\colon\{0,1\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
in a way similarly to the insertion-only algorithm (see Remark
10
). We store a pointer to the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
, as well as the parameters
t_{e},t_{0}
and
\gamma
. Using the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
, we reference random variables

\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}\sim[0,1]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{A}_{x,t}^{(r)}\sim[0,1]\qquad\text{for all $t\in[t_{e}]$ and $x\in[\Delta]^{d}$.}

Importantly, the algorithm can use the random oracle
\boldsymbol{r}
to access
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
or
\mathbf{A}_{x,t}^{(r)}
when given
x
and
t
without having to explicitly store them. As in Remark
10
, it will be clear from the way we use
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{(r)}
and
\mathbf{A}_{x,t}^{(r)}
that they may sampled and stored to finite precision. The algorithms are specified in Figure
12
.

Preprocessing Subroutines for

DS-E-Max-Cut
. We provide access to the memory contents of
DS-E-Max-Cut
after processing
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset[\Delta]^{d}
.

Build-Summ
()
. Outputs the correlated timeline-mask summary
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K})
.

1.

Initialize a set of tuples
\mathbf{P}
, initially empty, as well as a counter
c=1
.

2.

Iterate through

(a)

(b)

(c)

If it outputs

, insert

if

.

If it outputs “activated after

”, let

3.

Output

.

1.

If

If not, execute the sketch from Lemma

Lemma 7.5
.

An execution of

For

.

Proof:

in

As an intermediate claim, note that

From the above paragraph, Line

at most
