Preprocessing: Selecting the Seed.

From Lemma
7.5
, the dynamic streaming algorithm may sample a correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
from Definition
7.3
, build the summary
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K})
, and access the activation times
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
for each point
x_{i}\in X
. The algorithm can extract from
\mathbf{P}
the set
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
of points in
X
whose activation time occurred up to time
t_{0}
, and determine
m=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
. For any seed
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{m}
for
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
(Definition
2.4
), and any
x_{i}\in X
, the algorithm may execute
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
in Figure
11
for activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
as follows:

1.

First, the algorithm executes
\textsc{Activation-Time}(x_{i},t_{e},\mathbf{P})
, and obtains as output the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
, or “not activated by
t_{e}
.”

2.

Then, it either outputs
\textsc{Assign}_{\sigma,\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
, or
(1,0)
if “not activated by
t_{e}
.”

The seeds define a family of
2^{m}
cuts
z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
obtained by executing the above procedure for
z_{i}
. For any fixed cut
z
, and one may approximately estimate
f(z)
up to multiplicative error
(1+\varepsilon)
and additive error
\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})
; as in Lemma
A.2
, we union bound over all
2^{m}
cuts.
9
9

9

The reason we do not invoke Lemma
A.2
directly is because we do not have access to a random set
\mathbf{C}
as generated in that lemma, and because we will only have approximate access to the sampling probabilities.
Since
Build-Summ
()
(if it does not fail) uses at most
s/2
geometric sampling sketches (Lemma
7.1
) in Figure
12
, the algorithm still has
s/2
samples. It partitions samples into pairs; we denote the
h
-th successful pair when the samples
(\boldsymbol{y}_{h,1}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{h,1}^{*})
and
(\boldsymbol{y}_{h,2}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{h,2}^{*})
are both not
\bot
(which occurs for any individual pair with probability at least
1/\mathsf{D}^{2}
). Let
\boldsymbol{i}_{h,1}
and
\boldsymbol{i}_{h,2}
be so
\boldsymbol{y}_{h,1}^{*}=x_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h,1}}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{h,2}^{*}=x_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h,2}}
. Letting
\mathbf{H}
be the total number of successful pairs,
\mathbf{H}
is stochastically dominated by
\operatorname{Bin}(s/4,1/\mathsf{D}^{2})
. Conditioned on any
\mathbf{H}=H
and it being realized by a fixed set
G\subset[s/4]
of successful pair of sketches of size
|G|=H
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})^{2}}\cdot f(z)\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\frac{1}{2\cdot H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\dfrac{d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h,1}},x_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h,2}})}{\boldsymbol{p}_{h,1}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{h,2}^{*}}\left(z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{1}},0}z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{2}},0}+z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{1}},1}z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{2}},1}\right)\right]\leq f(z),

where the expectation is over the realization of the samples
(\boldsymbol{y}_{\cdot}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{\cdot}^{*})
from Lemma
7.1
, and the approximate guarantee on the expectation is because
\boldsymbol{p}_{h,1}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{p}_{h,2}^{*}
are
(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})
-approximations of the probability that
\boldsymbol{y}_{h,1}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{h,2}^{*}
are sampled. For any fixed
H
, above estimator is an average of independent random variables, with the variance of the
h
-th summand at most

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Var}\/}\left[\dfrac{d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h,1}},x_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{2}}})}{\boldsymbol{p}_{h,1}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{h,2}^{*}}\left(z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{1}},0}z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{2}},0}+z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{1}},1}z_{\boldsymbol{i}_{h_{2}},1}\right)\right]\leq\mathsf{D}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{i}\cdot w_{j}},

where
w_{i}
is proportional to the sum of all distances in
X
to
x_{i}
. The same argument as in the proof of Lemma
A.1
implies that the above variance is at most

16\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)^{2},

and thus, whenever
\mathbf{H}
is larger than
O(m/\varepsilon^{2})
, we may obtain the desired approximation to
f(z)
for all
z\in\{0,1\}^{m}
. By selecting the minimizing
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
, we can select the best seed in
\{0,1\}^{m}
.

Proof of Theorem
17
: Putting Everything Together.

For the correctness of algorithm, it suffices to check that we can apply Theorem
16
. First, our algorithm initializes the sketch of Lemma
7.2
and the
s
samples of Lemma
7.1
. Both Lemma
7.5
and Lemma
7.1
, fail with probability at most
\varepsilon
(either by outputting “fail”, or in a manner undetectable to the algorithm); either way, we consider these catastrophic failure (and we upper bound its contribution later). If
Build-Summ
()
does not output “fail,” then it specifies a draw to a correlated timeline-mask
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
and
\mathbf{K}
from Definition
7.3
and outputs
\mathbf{P}=\textsc{Summ}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n},\mathbf{K})
; furthermore,
Activation-Time
(x_{i},t,\mathbf{P})
provides query access to the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
. We apply the algorithm from above to select the optimal seed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
, and we let
\textsc{Assign}(x)
output
(1,0)
or
(0,1)
according to the following process:

•

Use
Activation-Time
(x_{i},t_{e},\mathbf{P})
to obtain the activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
for
\mathbf{A}_{i}
if its activated by time
t_{e}
, or output
(1,0)
as a default if it outputs “not activated by
t_{e}
” if it does not get activated.

•

If the algorithm produced an activation time
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
, then output
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})

Since
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
approximately minimizes
f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\min_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{m}}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))\right]+O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\\
z_{i}\neq(0,0)\end{subarray}}f(z)+O(\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j}),

where the first line uses the fact that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
is chosen to minimize
f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))
, and the second inequality applies Theorem
16
.

8
Proof of Theorem
16

To prove Theorem
16
, we follow the same proof structure as of Theorem
4
incorporating the changes to the timeline and access to estimates of the sampling probability. As before, we first describe a randomized algorithm, which receives as input access to the metric space, certain parameters which describe the random process, and description of a minimizing cut
z^{*}
of
f
. However, there are two important differences which add some complication:

•

We will sample “activated” and “kept” points using Lemma
7.1
, which will produce
s
samples
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{j}
(
j\in[s]
) from a probability distribution which is
\mathrm{poly}(d\log\Delta/\varepsilon)
-metric compatible; furthermore, it outputs an approximation
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{j}
to the probability the point was sampled. This probability can be used for importance sampling, but it is only an estimate of the true probability. We must ensure that the errors incurred from this estimate do not accumulate.

•

Perhaps more apparent, the procedure no longer behaves independently for each point. There is a single mask
\mathbf{K}
, and whenever
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, at most a single point will have
\mathbf{A}_{x,t}=1
(i.e., that which is sampled from Lemma
7.1
).

Process.

We call the modified random process
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
with the following input/output behavior (designed to produce a correlated Timeline-Mask from Definition
7.3
):

•

Input
: access to the metric space
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
. We consider a fixed successful instance of Lemma
7.2
, and we let
w\in(0,1/2]^{n}
denote the vector where
w_{i}=\boldsymbol{w}(x_{i})/(2\mathsf{D})
. The times
t_{0},t_{e}\in\mathbbm{N}
, a parameter
\gamma\geq 1
, and a matrix
z^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
which will be a minimizer of
f
encoding cuts:

z^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}\left\{f(z):z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}\text{ where every $i\in[n]$ has $z_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}$}\right\}.

•

Output
: the process outputs a random (partial) cut
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
where
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1),(0,0)\}
for all
i\in[n]
. In addition, a number
\boldsymbol{m}\in\mathbbm{N}
, and a string
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\in\{0,1\}^{\boldsymbol{m}}
.

The algorithm is described below.

Description.

CorrelatedGreedyProcess
((X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d),w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e},z^{*})
proceeds in rounds
t=1,2,\dots,t_{e}
, and will
generate the following sequences of random variables:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{t}\text{: a matrix $\{0,1\}^{n\times\{0,1\}}$ which encodes a partial cut at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{t}\text{: a vector of $n$ weights at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{t}\text{: tuples $(x_{j},\ell;p_{\ell},t_{j})$ for indices $\ell\leq t$, values $p_{\ell}$, and $t_{j}\leq\ell$ where $\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}^{t}=1$.}

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{t}\text{: a set of assigned points at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}^{t}\text{: a binary $n\times\{0,1\}$ matrix which encodes the greedy assignment at time $t$.}

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{t}\text{: an $n\times t$ binary matrix.}

as well as a random binary vector
\mathbf{K}\in\{0,1\}^{t_{e}}
which determines how the process behaves at each time step. At the start, the process generates
\mathbf{K}
, by
iterating through each time
1\leq t\leq t_{e}
and setting
\gamma^{t}=1
when
t\leq t_{0}
and
\gamma^{t}=\min(1,\gamma/t)
otherwise. Then, sample
\mathbf{K}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
. Let
s
be the number of nonzero-entries in
\mathbf{K}
, e.g.
s=\|\mathbf{K}\|_{1}
. The algorithm then uses Lemma
7.1
to drawn
s
many samples from
\mathcal{G}_{s}(X)
. We re-index these samples, as described by Remark
15
.

We let
\boldsymbol{z}^{0}
be the all zero’s matrix,
\mathbf{A}^{0}
be the empty
n\times 0
matrices,
\mathbf{P}^{0}=\emptyset
, and
\mathbf{S}^{0}=\emptyset
. For time
1\leq t\leq t_{e}
, we instantiate
\mathbf{A}^{t}
:

1.

For each
i\in[n]
, if
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
we set
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
to
\min\{w_{i},1/t\}
. Otherwise we set
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
to
w_{i}
. For each
(x_{j},\ell;p_{\ell},t_{j})\in\mathbf{P}^{t-1}
, we let
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}=\min\{p_{\ell},1/\ell\}
if
\ell=t_{j}
and
p_{\ell}
if
\ell>t_{j}
.

2.

For each
i\in[n]
, if
t\leq t_{0}
let
\boldsymbol{g}^{t}_{i}=z^{*}_{i}
,
else for
t>t_{0}
, we let

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}^{t-1}}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\boldsymbol{z}_{j,0}^{t-1}

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}^{t-1}}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\boldsymbol{z}_{j,1}^{t-1};

even though
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}
is only defined for
(x_{j},\ell;p_{\ell},t_{j})\in\mathbf{P}^{t-1}
,
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}^{t-1}
is non-zero only for these. We let
\boldsymbol{g}^{t}_{i}
be set to
(1,0)
if
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}<\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}
or
(0,1)
otherwise.

3.

If
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, select the corresponding sample
(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})
, and let
i^{*}\in[n]
such that
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t}=x_{i^{*}}
.

•

If
i^{*}\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, sample
\boldsymbol{b}_{t}\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\min\{1/(t\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}),1\})
and set
\mathbf{A}_{i^{*},t}^{t}=\boldsymbol{b}_{t}
. Update
\mathbf{P}^{t}\leftarrow\mathbf{P}^{t-1}
and add
(x_{i^{*}},t;\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*},t)
if
\boldsymbol{b}_{t}=1
. For
j\neq i^{*}
,
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}=0
, and
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t}=\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}
for
\ell<t
.

•

If
i^{*}\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, find the activation time
t_{i^{*}}
. Update
\mathbf{P}^{t}\leftarrow\mathbf{P}^{t-1}\cup\{(x_{i^{*}},t;\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*},t_{i^{*}})\}
. Set
\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}={\bf 1}\{j=i^{*}\}
and
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t}=\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}
for
j<t
.

Finally, update
\mathbf{S}^{t}
to activated points
\mathbf{S}^{t-1}\cup\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{A}_{i,t}^{t}=1\}
by time
t
.

4.

For each
i\in[n]
, define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{t}_{i}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{z}^{t-1}_{i}&i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}\\
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}&i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}\land i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}\\
(0,0)&\text{else}\\
\end{cases}

5.

Let
\boldsymbol{m}=|\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}|
, and consider a natural ordering
\pi
where
\pi_{j}
is the
j
-th element of
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
. Let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}^{*}=\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{\pi_{i}}

When
t>t_{e}
, the process completes and we output
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
, and we will refer to
\mathbf{A}^{t_{e}}
and
\mathbf{P}^{t_{e}}
as
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{P}
respectively.

One complication is that geometric samples
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*})
only provide approximate access to the true probabilities from which they are sampled, and only for the point which is sampled. We define the following variables which help us keep track of the quantities that the algorithm knows or does not know.

Definition 8.1
.

Consider an execution of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
with the parameters
((\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d),w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e},z^{*})
. Then, for any time
t
where
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, consider the set of samples
\{(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*})\}
from Lemma
7.1
. With probability
1-\delta
the initialization succeeds and we define the following:

•

For
i\in[n]
,
p_{i}^{t}
is the probability over
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*})
that
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
, which by Lemma
7.1
(see Remark
14
) satisfies

p_{i}^{t}=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}\right]\geq\frac{1}{\mathsf{D}}\cdot\dfrac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}d(x_{j},x_{j^{\prime}})}.

The algorithm does not observe
p_{i}^{t}
, but for
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i^{*}}
,
p_{i^{*}}^{t}\leq\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}\leq(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})p_{i^{*}}^{t}
.

•

For
i^{*}\in[n]
with
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i^{*}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{i^{*}}^{t}
is set to
\min\{\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*},1/t\}
if
i^{*}\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
(i.e., first activated at
t
), and
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}
otherwise. The algorithm does know
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{i^{*}}^{t}
.

•

For
i^{*}\in[n]
with
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i^{*}}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i^{*}}^{t}
is set to
p_{i^{*}}^{t}\cdot\min\{1/(t\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}),1\}
if
i^{*}\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, and
p_{i^{*}}^{t}
otherwise.

For an execution of the first
t-1
steps and
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, the probability that
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
is
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}
, which is at most
1/t
since
\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*}\geq p_{i}^{t}
whenever
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
.

Connecting
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
and
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{P}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Similarly to
GreedyProcess
, we have designed
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
so that
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
contains all activated points by
t_{0}
(which are all in
\mathbf{P}
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
specifies the optimal cut assignment for
\mathbf{S}^{t_{0}}
(analogous to Observation
11
and
12
). The only minor difference is Observation
13
, which incorporates the geometric samples.

Observation 18
.

For every
i\in[n]
, let
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
denote the smallest number where
\mathbf{A}_{i,\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}=1
. Then,
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}=\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
, and
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
depends on the values
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{\boldsymbol{t}_{i}}
, which may be computed from:

•

The point
x_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
,

•

The tuples
(x_{j},\ell;\boldsymbol{p}_{\ell}^{*},\boldsymbol{t}_{j})\in\mathbf{P}
for
\ell\leq\boldsymbol{t}_{i}-1
which contain the point
x_{j}
, the time
\ell
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
, the sampling weight
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{\ell}
, and the smallest
\boldsymbol{t}_{j}
where
\mathbf{A}_{j,\boldsymbol{t}_{j}}=1
, and
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}
(which may be recursively computed from
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}
and
\mathbf{P}
).

Similarly to
GreedyProcess
, the final vector
\boldsymbol{z}
is set by
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
.

8.1
Proof of Theorem
16
assuming Lemma
8.2

Lemma 8.2

(Main Lemma)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})
be the output of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
((X,d),

w,\gamma,t_{0},t_{e},z^{*})
, where the weights
w
are
\mathsf{D}
-compatible. For any
\varepsilon>0
, if

\gamma\geq\dfrac{(\mathsf{D}\ln(t_{e})+1)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad t_{0}\geq\max\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma}\cdot\mathsf{D}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\ln(t_{e})}.

Then,

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})]\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

Proof:
[Proof of Theorem
16
assuming Lemma
8.2
] Notice that, by construction
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is sampled from
\operatorname{Ber}(\gamma^{t})
which is
1
if
t\leq t_{0}
and
\min\{\gamma/t,1\}
otherwise; hence it is distributed exactly as
\mathcal{K}(t_{0},\gamma)
. This now imposes that each column of
\mathbf{A}
has it’s entries either behave independently and distributed solely based on
w_{i}
and
t
, or dependently, such that there is at most one activation, based on a sample
(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})
from
\mathcal{G}_{t}(X)
and
t
.
In particular, note that when
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
,
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is always sampled from
\operatorname{Ber}(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t})
, where the setting of
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}
depends solely on whether
i
is or is not in
\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
; if it is not in
\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, it is set to
\min\{w_{i},1/t\}
and otherwise
w_{i}
.

\mathbf{K}_{i,t}=1
,
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is depends on a sample from
(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})\sim\mathcal{G}_{t}(X)
from Lemma
7.1
. Notice that if
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, then
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
with probability
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}=p_{i}^{t}\cdot\min\left\{1/(t\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t}),1\right\}
, where
\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t}
is within
(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})
of
p_{i}^{t}
; if
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, then
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
with probability
p_{i}^{t}
. Note that
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
if and only if there exists
\ell\leq t-1
with
\mathbf{A}_{i,\ell}=1
, matching Definition
7.3
. Observe that the set
\mathbf{P}
depends solely on
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{K}
, and since those two distributions match, the distribution of the sets
\mathbf{P}
are identical, matching Definition
7.4
. If we let
\boldsymbol{z}
denote the cut which was output by greedy process, we partition the coordinates into

\mathbf{G}=\left\{i\in[n]:\exists t\leq t_{e}\text{ s.t }\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{B}=[n]\setminus\mathbf{G}.

Importantly, we have defined
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
and
Assign
so that, whenever
i\in\mathbf{G}
, if we let
\boldsymbol{t}_{i}
denote the activation time of point
x_{i}
, we have
\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*})_{i}
, which is the output of
\textsc{Assign}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{P}}(x_{i},\boldsymbol{t}_{i})
is the same as
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[\min_{\sigma}f(\boldsymbol{z}(\sigma))\right]\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{K}}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right)\cdot\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[i\in\mathbf{B}\right].

(25)

The final bound follows from Lemma
8.3
, where we upper bound

\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}[i\in\mathbf{B}]=\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}[i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t_{e}}]\leq\frac{\mathsf{D}}{w_{i}\cdot t_{e}}\cdot e^{2},

as long as
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq 1/\ln(t_{e})
. As in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, recall that
1/w_{i}\geq 1/(2n)
(recall, Lemma
A.1
), so that we may upper bound the above probability by
O(\mathsf{D}n/t_{e})
. Letting
t_{e}
be a large enough constant factor of
O(\mathsf{D}n/\varepsilon)
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq 1/\ln(t_{e})
gives the desired bound.

Lemma 8.3
.

Consider any execution of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
, as well as a fixed point
x_{i}\in X
, and any time
t
, as long as
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq 1/\ln t
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}\right]\leq\frac{\mathsf{D}}{w_{i}\cdot t}\cdot e^{2}

Proof:

For any fixed
\mathbf{K}
, every time
\ell
where
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=0
and
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}
, the probability that
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
is exactly
1-\min\{w_{i},1/\ell\}
; if
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
and
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}
, the probability that
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
is
1-p_{i}^{\ell}\cdot\min\{1/(\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{\ell}\cdot\ell),1\}
which is at most
1-\min\{p_{i}^{\ell},1/(\ell(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}))\}
(see Definition
8.1
).

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}\right]\leq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=0\end{subarray}}^{t}(1-\min\{w_{i},1/\ell\})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1\end{subarray}}^{t}\left(1-\min\left\{p_{i}^{\ell},\frac{1}{\ell(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})}\right\}\right)

For every
i\in[n]
, let
p_{i}
denote the minimum of
w_{i}
and
p_{i}^{\ell}
for all
\ell
with
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}\right]\leq\prod_{\ell=\lceil 1/p_{i}\rceil}^{t}\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell}\right)\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=\lceil 1/p_{i}\rceil\\
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1\end{subarray}}^{t}\left(\dfrac{1-\frac{1}{(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})\ell}}{1-\frac{1}{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{\lceil 1/p_{i}\rceil-1}{t}\cdot\exp\left(2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}1/\ell\right)

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{\lceil 1/p_{i}\rceil-1}{t}\cdot e^{2}

whenever
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq 1/\ln(t)
. The final bound comes from the fact
p_{i}\geq w_{i}/\mathsf{D}
.

9
Proof of Lemma
8.2

In the analysis to follow, it is important to establish the following definitions:

•

We let
w\in(0,1/2]^{n}
denote the vector specified by
w_{i}=\boldsymbol{w}(x_{i})
produced by a single execution of Lemma
7.2
which succeeds. We shall therefore consider a fixed instance of Lemma
7.2
which succeeds, so the weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in(0,1/2]^{n}
are fixed (and not random, so unbolded).

•

In an execution of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
, whenever
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, we utilize an instance of Lemma
7.1
to generate the corresponding geometric sample. For
t
with
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, we define
p_{i}^{t}
as the probability that the instance of Lemma
7.1
used at time
t
generates
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})
with
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
.

•

For
t
with
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, we define
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}
as the probability that
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
(Definition
8.1
). Note
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}
depends on whether or not
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
. When
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}=p_{i}^{t}
, since
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
when
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{*})
satisfies
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
. However, if
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, then
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}
is given by the expectation of
p_{i}^{t}\cdot\min\{1/(t\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t}),1\}
over
(\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})
from Lemma
7.1
with
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
. Importantly, Lemma
7.1
implies that anytime
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
, we will have
p_{i}^{t}\leq\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t}\leq(1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})p_{i}^{t}
, and this implies
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}\leq 1/t
.

We define an analogous fictitious cut, where we are careful to consider the various settings of
\mathbf{K}_{t}
in order to update in the case we do not activate at a particular time.

Definition 9.1

(Fictitious cut)
.

The fictitious cut is specified by a sequence of random variables
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}\in[0,1]^{n\times\{0,1\}}
for each
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{e}\}
. We let:

•

For
t=0
, we define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{0}=z^{*}
.

•

For
t>0
, we denote for each
i\in[n]
, the vector
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}\in[0,1]^{\{0,1\}}
by
10
10

10

As in Section
6
, it is not a prior clear that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}\in[0,1]
but we will establish this fact.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}^{t}&i\in\mathbf{S}^{t}\\
\dfrac{1}{1-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}}\left(\dfrac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}\right)&\text{ $\mathbf{K}_{t}=1$}\\
\dfrac{1}{1-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}}\left(\dfrac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{t}\right)&\text{ $\mathbf{K}_{t}=0$}\end{array}\right.

As in Section
6
, it is immediate to check the analogous version to Claim
6.2
. Namely, that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}=z^{*}
for
t\in\{0,\dots,t_{0}\}
and that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t_{e}}_{i,b}\geq\boldsymbol{z}_{i,b}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
. Furthermore, we similarly can re-write
f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})
into the various types of terms (
17
), (
18
) and (
19
). We note that the first statement of Lemma
6.3
, that for any execution of the first
t-1
steps,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right]=\frac{1}{t}\cdot\left(g_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)

(29)

remains true (by considering the two cases of
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
or
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
. However, the second statement of Lemma
6.3
still holds, albeit with a different argument and constant factor loss, since the proof of Lemma
6.3
crucially relies on separate points being independent. Even though we no longer have independence, we state a lemma which will help us handle this.

Lemma 9.2
.

For any
0\leq p_{1},p_{2}\leq 1
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathbbm{R}
, then consider the following joint distribution over random variables
(\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2})
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}\sim\begin{cases}\mathbf{X}_{1}=\alpha_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}=\beta_{2}&\text{ w.p. }p_{1}\\
\mathbf{X}_{1}=\beta_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}=\alpha_{2}&\text{ w.p. }p_{2}\\
\mathbf{X}_{1}=\beta_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}=\beta_{2}&\text{ w.p. }1-p_{1}-p_{2}\end{cases}.

Then,
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}]=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}]\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{2}]+p_{1}p_{2}(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-\beta_{1}\beta_{2})
.

Proof:

The desired property follows from a simple calculation of
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}]
and
\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}]\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{2}]

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}]

\displaystyle=p_{1}\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+p_{2}\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}+(1-p_{1}-p_{2})\beta_{1}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=p_{1}p_{2}(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+\beta_{1}\alpha_{2})+p_{1}(1-p_{2})\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+p_{2}(1-p_{1})\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}+(1-p_{1}-p_{2})\beta_{1}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=p_{1}p_{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}+p_{1}(1-p_{2})\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+p_{2}(1-p_{1})\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}+(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})\beta_{1}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+p_{1}p_{2}(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-\beta_{1}\beta_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}]\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{2}]+p_{1}p_{2}(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-\beta_{1}\beta_{2})

Lemma 9.3
.

We have that for any
t\geq t_{0}
and any
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right)\right]\leq\frac{3}{t^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}).

Proof:

Consider any fixed execution of the first
t-1
steps of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
which fixes the setting of
\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}
and
g_{i,b}^{t}
the setting of
w_{i}^{t}
(in Line
1
), and
g_{i,b}^{t}
(in Line
2
), as these depend only on the randomness which occurred up to step
t-1
). We now consider what happens at step
t
, where we apply linearity of expectation to consider fixed
i,j
, and it suffices to only consider
i,j\notin S^{t-1}
, since
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}=\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}
if
i\in S^{t-1}
. With probability
1-\gamma^{t}
, we have
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
, and in this case
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}
are independent. Thus, conditioned on
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
, we have (as in Lemma
6.3
)

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right)\mid\mathbf{K}_{t}=0\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2t^{2}}\sum_{i\notin S^{t-1}}\sum_{j\notin S^{t-1}}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(g_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)\left(g_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2t^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}),

where we first use (
29
) and then the fact
g_{i,b}^{t},\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\in[0,1]
. It remains to consider the case
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, where the challenge is that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}
are no longer independent. However, here we use Lemma
9.2
with
\mathbf{X}_{1}
set to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{2}
set to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}
for
i,j\notin S^{t-1}
. Using the notation of Lemma
9.2
, we associate
\alpha_{1}
with the event that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}=g_{i,b}^{t}
when
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})\sim\mathcal{G}_{t}(X)
happens to have
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{i}
, and similarly
\beta_{1}
with the event that
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{t})\sim\mathcal{G}_{t}(X)
has
\boldsymbol{y}^{*}_{t}=x_{j}
. Note
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in[-1,1]
(because
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t},\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t},\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\in[0,1]
), and we have always required
\alpha_{1}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}\leq 1/t
and
\beta_{1}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{t}\leq 1/t
. By Lemma
9.2
, the cases
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}-\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right)\mid\mathbf{K}_{t}=1\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}+\frac{4}{t^{2}}\right).

As in Section
6
, Lemma
9.3
is used to upper bound terms analogous to (
18
) and (
19
), it remains to upper bound terms analogous to (
17
).

Definition 9.4

(Contribution Matrix, Ideal Estimated Contribution, and (Non-Ideal) Estimated Contribution)
.

For time
t\in\{1,\dots,t_{e}\}
, the contribution matrix
\boldsymbol{c}^{t}\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
is given by letting,

\boldsymbol{c}^{t}_{i,0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j,0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{c}^{t}_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{j,1}.

For a time
t\in\{1,\dots,t_{e}\}
and an index
j\in[n]
, we define
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{0}=1
and for
t\geq 2

\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t-1}=\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}=\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}},

and ideal estimated contribution matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
be given by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}_{i,0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t-1}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t-1}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t-1},

and the (non-ideal) estimated contribution matrix
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}^{n\times\{0,1\}}
be given by

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}_{i,0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}^{t}_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t-1},

and notice that, since
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,0}^{t}=\boldsymbol{z}_{j,0}^{t}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,1}^{t}=\boldsymbol{z}_{j,1}^{t}
when
j\in\mathbf{S}^{t}
(and equivalently, when
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t}
is non-zero), we defined
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}
to align with Line
2
in
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
.

Recall that similarly to Section
6
, we upper bound for every
t\in\{t_{0}+1,\dots,t_{e}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t})-f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right]

(30)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{6}{t^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}),

(31)

using Lemma
9.3
to upper bound (
31
). Hence, it suffices to upper bound the expectation in (
30
). We re-write the terms inside the expectation as a sum of three terms and use (
29
) to consider the expectation solely over the randomness in the
t
-th step:

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,t},\mathbf{K}_{t}}\left[\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{i,b}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t-1}\right]=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,b}^{t-1}\right)\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}\right|+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}\right|+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,b}\right)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}.

(32)

Where we use that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}
when
i\in\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, and the fact that
\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t},\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{t-1}\in[0,1]
. As in Section
6
, we upper bound the three terms in (
32
) individually, using the following three lemmas.

Lemma 9.5
.

For any execution of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
, any
t\in\{t_{0}+1,\dots,t_{e}\}
and any
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
,

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,0}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}+\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{i,1}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t-1}_{i,1}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,1}^{t}\leq 0.

The proof of the lemma follows in the same way as Lemma
6.5
, where we first show
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}=1
and
|\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,0}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i,1}^{t}|\leq 1
, and the only change is that one must consider the case
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
separately, where each
w_{i}^{t},\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}\leq 1/t
. This allows us to upper bound the last term in (
32
). The middle term is bounded by the following lemma, simply using the fact that
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}
will be multiplicatively close to each other.

Lemma 9.6
.

For any
t\in\{t_{0}+1,\dots,t_{e}\}
, any
i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}
, and any
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}|\right]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})

Proof:

From Definition
9.4
, we have that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t-1}\in[0,1]
. Recall, furthermore that whenever
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
and we use a sample
(\boldsymbol{y}_{\ell}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{\ell}^{*})
from Lemma
7.1
, a setting of
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}=1
means
\boldsymbol{y}_{\ell}^{*}=x_{j}
. In this case
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}=\min\{\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{\ell},1/\ell\}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}=p_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\min\{1/(\ell\boldsymbol{p}^{*}_{\ell}),1\}
. This means, in particular, that
|\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{*}/{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}-1|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,0}^{t}\right|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left|\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t-1}-\mathbf{R}_{j}^{t-1}\right|\leq\frac{1}{t-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\cdot\left|1-\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{j}^{\ell}}\right|,

which simplifies to
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})
times
1/(t-1)\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}^{t-1}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}/(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}\gamma^{\ell})
, and once we take expectation over
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}
, we obtain the desired bound.

The final lemma remaining is that which bounds the first term of (
32
). This is the lemma analogous to Lemma
6.10
from Section
6
.

Lemma 9.7
.

For any
t\in\{2,\dots,t_{e}\}
and any
b\in\{0,1\}
, whenever
\|p^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t-1}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}\right|\right]\leq 10e\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\gamma}}{t-1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}.

Proof:
[Proof of Lemma
8.2
assuming Lemmas
9.5
,
9.6
and
9.7
]
The proof follows analogously to that of Lemma
5.1
in Section
6
. In particular, we bound

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[f(\boldsymbol{z})-f(z^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(O(1/t_{0})+O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}})\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{t_{e}}\frac{10e\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}}{t}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\gamma}}{t-1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}.

The second term above simplifies to

O(\mathsf{D})\left(\frac{\sqrt{\gamma}}{t_{0}}+\dfrac{\ln t_{e}}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})\right),

which gives the desired bound once
\gamma=(\mathsf{D}\ln t_{e}+1)^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
, and
t_{0}=O(\mathsf{D}\sqrt{\gamma}/\varepsilon)
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{pr}}\leq\varepsilon/\ln t_{e}
.

Proof of Lemma
9.7

The proof will be similar to Lemma
6.6
in Section
6
. We will similarly re-index
t
so that the definition of
\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}
depends solely on the randomness up to step
t
, i.e., those from
\mathbf{A}^{t}_{j,\ell},\mathbf{K}_{\ell}
for all
j\neq i
and
\ell\leq t
. We let

\displaystyle\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t}\right)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}_{j,b}^{t}=d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(1-\frac{\mathbf{A}_{j,t}^{t}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{t}}{t\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{t}\gamma^{t}}-\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{j}^{t-1}\right)\cdot\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{t}_{j,b}

\displaystyle\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)=\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}.

By linearity of expectation, Jensen’s inequality, we seek to upper bound

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}|\right]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1})^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2}.

(33)

The following lemma is analogous to Lemma
6.8
in Section
6
. As in Section
6
, we have defined the fictitious cut so as to satisfy the conclusion of Lemma
6.8
. The only minor subtlety is that one must consider the case
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
and
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
separately, since the fictitious cut and the probability that
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}=1
differs in each case (when
\mathbf{K}_{t}=0
,
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is
1
with probability
w_{i}^{t}
; when
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
,
\mathbf{A}_{i,t}
is
1
with probability
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{t}
). Considering both cases, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 9.8
.

For any execution of the first
t-1
steps of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
, fixing the random variables
A^{t-1}
and any setting of the first
t
values
K_{\ell}
for
\ell\leq t
, and
\widehat{z}^{t-1}
. Then, considering only the
t
-th step of
CorrelatedGreedyProcess
,

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\mathbf{A}_{\cdot,t}}\left[\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)\right]=\frac{t-1}{t}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{t}(j),

where the expectation is taken only with respected to the randomness in the
t
-th step.

Lemma 9.9
.

For any
t\in\{2,\dots,t_{e}-1\}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\ell^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2}.

The proof of the above claim also follows similarly to Lemma
6.9
in Section
6
. The one thing to check that the final step in which Lemma
6.9
, which uses independence of
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j)
and
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{t+1}(j^{\prime})
may still be done even though these are not independent. In particular, Lemma
6.9
analogously establishes

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t+1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t+1}\right|\right]\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\ell^{2}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2},

but we must verify that, even though differing values of
j
are not independent,

\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right].

In particular, we have the following claim.

Claim 9.10
.

For any fixed setting of the first
\ell-1
time steps, any two differing
j,j^{\prime}\in[n]
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j^{\prime})-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j^{\prime})\right)\right]\leq 0.

Proof:

For any setting of the first
\ell-1
time steps, for any of the settings of
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}
, the expectation of
\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-(\ell-1)/\ell\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)
is zero from Lemma
9.8
. Whenever
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=0
, the two terms are independent and we obtain our desired bound. When
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
, the terms are non-independent. We let

\mathbf{X}_{1}=\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{X}_{2}=\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j^{\prime})-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j^{\prime}).

We claim that, whenever
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}=1
(i.e., the
j
-th point is activated), we will have
\mathbf{X}_{1}<0
(and similarly,
\mathbf{A}_{j^{\prime},\ell}=1
implies
\mathbf{X}_{2}<0
). This suffices to prove the claim for the following reason: once we condition on
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
, we use Lemma
9.2
with
\alpha_{1}
be the setting of
\mathbf{X}_{1}
when
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}=1
, and we let
\alpha_{2}
be the setting of
\mathbf{X}_{2}
when
\mathbf{A}_{j^{\prime},\ell}=1
.
\mathbf{X}_{1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{2}
are both zero centered and supported on just two outcomes, so if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\leq 0
, then
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\geq 0
. By Lemma
9.2
, we have for any
p_{1},p_{2}\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}[\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}]=p_{1}p_{2}\left(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}+\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\right)\leq 0.

We verify that
\mathbf{X}_{1}<0
when
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}=1
(the case of
\mathbf{X}_{2}
is analogous), and we do so by consider the two cases:

•

If
j\in S^{\ell-1}
, then we know
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\ell}_{j,b}=\widehat{z}^{\ell-1}_{j,b}
. Thus, if
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
, we have that

\mathbf{X}_{1}=\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)=\frac{d(x_{i},x_{j})}{\ell}\left(1-\frac{1}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}\gamma^{\ell}}\right)\widehat{z}^{\ell-1}_{j,b}\leq 0

because
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}
and
\gamma^{\ell}
are both at most
1
, and
\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{\ell-1}\geq 0
.

•

If
j\notin S^{\ell-1}
, then we know
\widehat{R}_{j}^{\ell-1}=0
. Thus, if
\mathbf{A}_{j,\ell}\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\ell}=1
, we have that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\ell}_{j,b}=g_{j,b}^{\ell}\geq 0
, and

\mathbf{X}_{1}=\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)=d(x_{i},x_{j})\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)g_{j,b}^{\ell}-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\widehat{z}^{\ell-1}_{j,b}\right)\leq 0

because
\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}^{\ell}\leq 1/\ell
,
\gamma^{\ell}\leq 1
, and
\widehat{z}_{j,b}^{t-1}\geq 0
.

Lemma 9.11
.

Fix any
\ell>1
and any
i,j\in[n]
with
w_{j}\leq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 100\cdot\mathsf{D}\cdot e^{2}\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}\cdot\dfrac{1}{\ell^{2}\cdot w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}.

Proof:

The proof of the lemmas analogous to Lemma
6.10
in Section
6
, (note that when
\mathbf{K}_{t}=1
, we use the geometric sample
(\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*},\boldsymbol{p}_{t}^{*})
which satisfies
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}^{*}=x_{j}
with probability
p_{j}^{t}
),

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\mathbf{err}_{i,b}^{\ell+1}(j)-\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{err}_{i,b}^{\ell}(j)\right)^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot p_{j}^{\ell}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}+{\bf 1}\left\{j\notin S^{\ell-1}\right\}\cdot\frac{30\cdot d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{\ell^{2}\cdot\min\{p_{j}^{\ell},1/\ell\}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}.

and recall that we also have
p_{i}^{\ell}\geq w_{i}/\mathsf{D}
.
As in Lemma
6.10
, when we average over times up to
\ell-1
, we invoke Lemma
8.3
to upper bound

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[j\notin\mathbf{S}^{\ell-1}\right]\leq\frac{\mathsf{D}\cdot e^{2}}{w_{i}\cdot(\ell-1)},

and this gives us the final bound.

We complete the proof as in Lemma
6.6
, where we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{S}^{t}}\left|\boldsymbol{c}_{i,b}^{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i,b}^{t}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(100\mathsf{D}\cdot e^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}\cdot\gamma^{\ell}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 10e\sqrt{\mathsf{D}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\gamma}}{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{d(x_{i},x_{j})^{2}}{w_{j}}\right)^{1/2}.

Appendix A
Algorithmic Preliminaries

A.1
For Computing Metric-Compatible Weights

Our first ingredients are the sketching tools to compute metric compatible weights. The idea will be to apply the turnstile streaming algorithm for cascaded norms
[
JW09
]
, and is useful for obtaining a set of weights
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
for instances which are subsets of
\ell_{p}
for
p\in[1,2]
. When the input
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
consist of vectors in
\ell_{p}^{d}
for
p\in[1,2]
, we will use linear sketching in order to recover weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
which satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{k},x_{j})}\leq w_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{\ell_{p}}(x_{k},x_{j})}.

(34)

We first state the lemma describing the linear sketch for
\ell_{k}(\ell_{p})
, and then show that if we find a setting of weights satisfying (
34
), these are
O(1)
-compatible weights (which follows from an argument in
[
dlVK01
]
).

Theorem 19

(Sketching
\ell_{k}(\ell_{p})

[
JW09
]
)
.

For any fixed
n,d\in\mathbbm{N}
, any
1\leq k\leq p\leq 2
, and any
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, let
s=\mathrm{poly}(\log(nd/\delta)/\varepsilon)
. There exists a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
s\times nd
matrices such that, for any
n\times d
matrix
A
:

•

Draw
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
and let
\mathbf{sk}(A)=\mathbf{S}\cdot\mathrm{vec}(A)
, where
\mathrm{vec}(A)\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd}
is the flattened matrix.

•

There is a deterministic algorithm which receives as input
\mathbf{sk}(A)
and outputs a number
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}
which satisfies

\|A\|_{\ell_{k}(\ell_{p})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|A_{i}\|_{p}^{k}\right)^{1/k}\leq\boldsymbol{\eta}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\|A\|_{\ell_{k}(\ell_{p})},

where
A_{i}\in\mathbbm{R}^{d}
is the
i
-th row of
A
, with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
.

Furthermore, the draw
\mathbf{S}\sim\mathcal{D}
may be generated using
\mathrm{poly}(s)
random bits.

Lemma A.1

(Lemma 7 in
[
dlVK01
]
and Lemma 3.5 in
[
CJK23
]
)
.

Let
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
be any set of points in a metric
d
, let
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in[0,1]
satisfy

\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{j})}\leq w_{i}\leq\mathsf{D}\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{j})}.

Then, every
w_{i}\geq 1/(2n)
and any
c
in the metric satisfies:

\displaystyle\dfrac{d(c,x_{j})}{w_{j}^{\prime}}\leq 4\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(c,x_{k}).

Letting
w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{n}^{\prime}\in(0,1/2]
be
w_{j}^{\prime}=w_{j}/2
, we satisfy
\|w^{\prime}\|_{1}\leq\mathsf{D}/2
and
w^{\prime}
are
8
-compatible.

Proof:

We let
\ell\in[n]
denote the minimizer of
\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{\ell},x_{k})
, and we note that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(d(x_{k},x_{\ell})+d(x_{\ell},x_{t})\right)\leq 2n\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{\ell}),

(35)

and furthermore, every
i\in[n]
satisfies

w_{i}\geq\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{i})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})}\geq\dfrac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{\ell})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})}\geq\frac{1}{2n}.

Therefore, for any
c
,

\displaystyle\frac{d(c,x_{j})}{w_{j}^{\prime}}=2\cdot\frac{d(c,x_{j})}{w_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\frac{d(c,x_{j})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{j},x_{k})}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\left(\dfrac{\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{k}\sim[n]}\left[d(c,x_{\boldsymbol{k}})\right]}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{j},x_{k})}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})\right),

(36)

where we have used the triangle inequality to upper bound

d(c,x_{j})\leq\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{k}\sim[n]}\left[d(c,x_{\boldsymbol{k}})\right]+\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{k}\sim[n]}\left[d(x_{\boldsymbol{k}},x_{j})\right].

By the choice of
\ell
and (
35
), we can upper bound

\displaystyle\dfrac{\mathop{{\bf E}\/}_{\boldsymbol{k}\sim[n]}\left[d(c,x_{\boldsymbol{k}})\right]}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{j},x_{k})}\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\dfrac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(c,x_{k})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{\ell},x_{k})}\leq 2\cdot\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(c,x_{k})}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})}.

Thus, the right-hand side of (
36
) is at most

\displaystyle 4\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(c,x_{k})+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}d(x_{k},x_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(c,x_{k})+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(d(x_{k},c)+d(x_{t},c)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 8\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(c,x_{k}).

Finally, the fact
\|w^{\prime}\|_{1}\leq\mathsf{D}/2
follows from the fact
\|w\|_{1}\leq\mathsf{D}
.

A.2
For Choosing the Best Seed

The final algorithmic component will provide a means to determine the minimizing seed
\sigma
to use for Theorem
4
. Note that Theorem
4
argues about the expected minimum over a family of cuts. Namely, for
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{n}
, we let
\boldsymbol{m}
denote the number of indices
i\in[n]
which contain a non-zero entry within the first
t_{0}
entries. Then, Theorem
4
provides a family of at most
2^{\boldsymbol{m}}
cuts, one for each possible
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, and the minimizing
\sigma
provides an approximate max-cut. Here, we state a lemma which enables us to estimate the quality of the cut given local access. Algorithmically, we use this primitive in order to “agree” on a setting of
\sigma
.

Lemma A.2

(Seed Check)
.

For any
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, fix a metric
(X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\},d)
and let
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})\in\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
be
\lambda
-metric compatible weights with
\lambda\geq 1
and
\|w\|_{1}=O(1)
. For a family of cut assignments
\mathcal{F}
, let
\xi\geq\mathrm{poly}(\lambda\log(n|\mathcal{F}|/\delta)/\varepsilon)
. There exists a randomized algorithm that given

1.

A random subset
\mathbf{C}\subset X
, where
x_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
independently with probability
\min\{\xi\cdot w_{i},1\}
.

2.

For each
x_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
, access to the sampling probability
\min\{\xi\cdot w_{i},1\}
.

3.

A family
\mathcal{F}
of cut assignments
z\in\mathcal{F}
where
z\in\{(0,1),(0,1)\}^{n}
, and query access to
z_{i}
for all
x_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
.

The algorithm outputs with probability
1-\delta
over
\mathbf{C}
, an
\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathcal{F}
such that

\displaystyle f(\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}})\leq\min_{z\in\mathcal{F}}f(z)+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j}),

for
f
in (
2
).

Proof:

The algorithm proceeds by iterating over
z\in\mathcal{F}
, and using the sample in
\mathbf{C}
in order to estimate
f(z)
. Then, we will take a union bound over all
\mathcal{F}
and choose the minimum. For each
i\in[n]
, we let
\tilde{w}_{i}=\min\{\xi\cdot w_{i},1\}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}
denote the indicator random variable that
x_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
,
B_{ij}=d(x_{i},x_{j})\cdot(z_{i,0}z_{j,0}+z_{i,1}z_{j,1})/2
,
T_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(x_{i},x_{j})
and
T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{i}
. Notice then, that
f(z)
is the sum of all
B_{ij}
. It suffices to show that for any
z\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\mathbf{C}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{j}}{\tilde{w}_{i}\cdot\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}-f(z)\right|\leq\varepsilon T

with probability
1-\delta/|\mathcal{F}|
. Standard concentration bounds (namely, Bernstein’s inequality) do not directly apply because the summands
\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{j}/(\tilde{w}_{i}\cdot\tilde{w}_{j})\cdot B_{ij}
are not all independent. Let
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}_{i}-\tilde{w}_{i}
, so that expanding the definitions and symmetry in
B_{ij}
gives the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\mathbf{C}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{j}}{\tilde{w}_{i}\cdot\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}-f(z)\right|\geq\varepsilon T\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dfrac{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{i}\cdot\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon T}{2}\right]

(37)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{i}}{\tilde{w}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}B_{ij}-f(z)\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon T}{4}\right].

(38)

The probability in (
38
) is simple to bound via Bernstein’s inequality: first, we note that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{{\bf E}\/}\left[\left(\frac{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot T_{i}}{\tilde{w}_{i}}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n]\\
\tilde{w_{i}}<1\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{T_{i}^{2}}{\xi w_{i}}+T_{i}^{2}\right)\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{T_{i}^{2}}{\xi w_{i}}\leq\frac{2\lambda}{\xi}\cdot T^{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\max_{i:\tilde{w_{i}}<1}\frac{T_{i}}{\tilde{w_{i}}}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\xi}\cdot T,

hence, Bernstein’s inequality implies the probability in (
38
) is at most

\displaystyle 2\exp\left(-\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\xi}{32\cdot\lambda\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)}\right)\leq\frac{\delta}{2|\mathcal{F}|}

whenever
\xi
is a large enough constant times
\lambda\log(|\mathcal{F}|/\delta)/\varepsilon^{2}
. It then remains to upper bound (
37
), which we do using the following argument. Note that we can always upper bound

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{i}\cdot\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{i\in\mathbf{C}}\frac{1-\tilde{w}_{i}}{\tilde{w}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|+\left|\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{C}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{|\mathbf{C}|\cdot\max_{i}\frac{1}{\xi w_{i}}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|}_{A}+\underbrace{\left|\sum_{i\notin\mathbf{C}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|}_{B}.

Thus, whenever (
37
) occurs, at least of the two summands,
A
or
B
, is greater than
\varepsilon T/4
. Suppose that (
37
) occurs with probability at least
\delta/(2|\mathcal{F}|)
, and that among those events, half of them have term
A
being at least
\varepsilon T/4
. Then, we note that since
|\mathbf{C}|
is a sum of independent random variables with expectation
O(\xi)
, a simple Chernoff bound shows that
|\mathbf{C}|
is larger than
O(\xi\log(|\mathcal{F}|/\delta))
except with probability
o(\delta/|\mathcal{F}|)
, and therefore, with probability at least
\delta/(8|\mathcal{F}|)
,

\displaystyle\max_{i}\frac{1}{\xi w_{i}}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\xi\log(|\mathcal{F}|/\delta)}\right)\cdot T,

However, we always have

\displaystyle\mathop{{\bf Pr}\/}\left[\exists i\in[n]:\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}}{\tilde{w}_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}\right|\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\log(|\mathcal{F}|/\delta)}\right)\cdot T\cdot w_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 2n\cdot\exp\left(-\Omega(1)\cdot\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\xi}{\log^{2}(|\mathcal{F}|/\delta)\cdot(\lambda^{3}+\varepsilon\lambda/3)}\right),

which is smaller than
\delta/(8|\mathcal{F}|)
whenever
\xi
is a large enough constant factor of
\lambda^{3}\log^{3}(|\mathcal{F}|/\delta)\log n/\varepsilon^{2}
. This would give a contradiction, so summand
B
must be at least
\varepsilon T/4
with probability at least
\delta/(4|\mathcal{F}|)
. This, too, gives a contradiction, since whenever this occurs, there must be some
i
where the magnitude of
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\prime}/\tilde{w_{j}}\cdot B_{ij}
exceeds
\varepsilon T_{i}/4
, and this probability is similarly bounded by

2n\cdot\exp\left(-\Omega(1)\cdot\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}\xi}{\lambda(1+\varepsilon/3)}\right).
